1|1: Creating a separate pool for irreplaceable data can make sense if you want different hardware characteristics and failure domains. For example, you might put that pool on higher-quality drives, a more conservative RAID layout like RAIDZ2 or RAIDZ3, and a different HBA or chassis, so a failure in your main pool or its controller is less likely to affect the critical data. However, a separate pool on the same physical machine is not a substitute for a real backup; it just lowers some risks and lets you tune redundancy and performance differently.  If your goal is to protect against accidental deletion, malware, or catastrophic loss of the NAS itself (power event, theft, fire, water damage), then an additional backup—ideally offline or off-site—is much more important. In most home or small lab setups, it is usually better to keep everything in a single well-designed pool and spend your money and effort on a robust backup strategy for the irreplaceable dataset: regular snapshots, replication to another system or cloud, and at least one copy that is not always online.
2|1: A separate pool is mainly useful when you want to isolate risk and behavior: different vdev layout, different disk types, or different performance and reliability profiles. For irreplaceable data, you could build that pool with more conservative choices (like slower, larger, more redundant vdevs, or entirely different disks and cabling) so it is less vulnerable to things like pool-wide corruption or a single HBA going bad.  In practice, though, nothing in ZFS replaces having at least one independent backup. Snapshots and replication within the same box are still at the mercy of the same hardware, power supply, and physical location. If your question is which is more important, additional pool vs backup, the backup wins almost every time. A sensible middle ground is one pool for everything but with tight snapshot and replication schedules for the critical dataset to a different box, cloud target, or removable disk you can rotate off-site.
3|1: If all pools live in the same chassis and share power, cooling, and environment, the benefit of separating irreplaceable data into its own pool is relatively modest. You gain configuration flexibility—like using higher redundancy levels, disabling compression or dedup if inappropriate, and controlling scrub schedules separately—but you do not get protection from threats like fire, theft, or a lightning strike.  An additional backup of that dataset—especially if it involves a different physical location, or at least media that is usually disconnected—protects you against those larger-scale events and against user error. So, from a risk management perspective, it is almost always better to prioritize creating and maintaining an independent backup than to rely on separate pools on the same box. You can still use one pool and fine-tune dataset properties there, then replicate critical datasets to an external target.
4|1: A separate pool is helpful if you expect very different usage patterns. For example, your bulk media pool might be optimized for sequential reads and decent capacity, while a pool dedicated to irreplaceable documents and photos might favor extra redundancy, smaller vdevs, and potentially mirrored SSDs for reliability and low latency. In such a design, that second pool can be more carefully curated, maybe scrubbed more often, and perhaps never used for experiments or jails that might stress it.  But none of that equals the safety of a true backup. Backups let you roll back from accidental deletion, ransomware, filesystem-level corruption, or a botched configuration, and they cover total device loss. If you must choose between building a new pool for critical data on the same host or setting up an off-box backup, the off-box backup usually has a much bigger effect on your overall resilience.
5|1: If you already have a robust backup solution in place, adding a separate pool for irreplaceable data can be a fine-tuning step rather than a core protection measure. It may simplify administration: all mission-critical datasets in one pool, with stricter snapshot policies, different replication targets, and maybe more conservative upgrades and testing. You can treat your main pool more aggressively (VMs, plugins, frequent changes) and keep the critical pool boring and stable.  However, if you do not yet have a second, independent copy of that irreplaceable data, the priority should be to create one, not to rearrange pools. Backups can be as simple as a periodic ZFS send to a USB disk you unplug and store elsewhere, or replication to another TrueNAS box. Only after that base layer of safety is covered does it make sense to worry about fine-grained separation into pools for policy or performance reasons.
6|1: Using a single large pool and relying on ZFS datasets and properties is usually simpler, and simplicity itself is a form of reliability. With one pool, capacity is shared automatically, you have fewer vdev layouts to track, and you avoid accidentally under-provisioning a small pool that later needs expansion in awkward ways. For many TrueNAS setups, this is the recommended model: one well-designed pool, plus snapshotting and replication for protection.  Creating a second pool adds complexity: another set of vdevs, another set of scrub schedules, and more room for configuration mistakes. Unless that second pool is truly isolated in terms of hardware and location—and therefore acting more like a backup—the extra complexity rarely beats just having good backups. For irreplaceable data, that typically means frequent snapshots and at least one backup that lives off the main system, potentially off-site.
7|1: Separate pools can improve your protection against certain types of correlated hardware failures. For instance, you might create a pool for irreplaceable data on drives from a different batch or vendor, with a separate controller and cabling. If a firmware bug or bad backplane wipes out your primary pool, there is a chance your critical pool survives because it is on a different path. This is a valid strategy when you cannot afford a completely separate backup system but can afford some extra disks and a second controller.  Even then, you should regard this more as risk reduction than as backup. It might protect you from some hardware faults, but it does nothing against user error, malicious software, or physical destruction of the entire box. When evaluating where to spend a limited budget, a second machine or removable backup media usually gives more practical safety than just splitting pools in one chassis.
8|1: The role of a separate pool in TrueNAS is more about policy and performance isolation than about fundamental data safety. You might give your irreplaceable data pool a very cautious layout, like multiple small RAIDZ2 vdevs with conservative utilization thresholds, while your bulk pool might run RAIDZ1 or larger vdevs to save space. This helps ensure that experimental workloads or heavy fragmentation in the bulk pool do not impact your critical pool as much.  But if you are asking in the context of protecting against losing irreplaceable data altogether, a backup system offers stronger guarantees. A second copy on independent hardware, with versioned snapshots, addresses almost all serious risk categories more directly. So, if you have to choose, set up additional backup first. Then, if you want operational benefits, you can consider splitting pools for administrative or performance reasons.
9|1: In a home or small office environment, a common and effective pattern is a single ZFS pool on the primary TrueNAS system combined with replication to a cheaper secondary system or external disk. In this model, the complexity stays low, and your primary safety mechanism is the fact that your irreplaceable data exists on two different devices, potentially in two different locations. For most people, this balance of simplicity and redundancy is superior to splitting one physical box into multiple pools.  A second pool can still be justified in niche cases: for example, mixing SSD-only workloads with spinning disks, or when you know some data absolutely must be stored with higher redundancy and never be affected by experiments on the rest of the system. But you should not think of that second pool as your backup. Treat it as a differently tuned storage tier, with a separate and explicit backup job still configured for the truly irreplaceable stuff.
10|1: If you think in terms of the 3-2-1 rule of backups—three copies of your data, on two different media types, with one copy off-site—creating a separate pool inside the same TrueNAS box does not move you much closer to that ideal. It is still the same site, same power, same general media type, and often the same administrative domain. Adding an additional backup, especially one that leaves the building or is at least offline most of the time, directly advances you toward that target and meaningfully lowers your risk.  Therefore, for irreplaceable data, a better strategy is usually: design a single, robust pool; create frequent snapshots of key datasets; replicate those snapshots to another system, cloud, or removable media; and optionally consider a second pool later if you need special performance or policy isolation. The real safety comes from having an independent copy, not from rearranging how you carve up disks on one box.
1|2: At your level, the biggest gains usually come from going deeper rather than just learning the next framework. Pick one or two areas and push toward genuine expertise: for example, performance optimization at scale, web security and threat modeling, or distributed systems design behind web apps. Take existing systems you built and profile them carefully, then systematically reduce latency and resource usage. Read RFCs and official specs (HTTP, TLS, OAuth, HTML, CSS, ECMAScript) to understand how the web truly works underneath the abstractions. This kind of depth lets you see simpler solutions, make better tradeoffs, and mentor others more effectively.
2|2: Shift some focus from coding to system design and architecture. Practice designing entire systems on paper or whiteboard: authentication flows, multi-tenant SaaS apps, event-driven architectures, or real-time collaboration tools. For each design, explicitly write down requirements, constraints, data models, scaling strategies, failure modes, and observability plans. Compare your designs with open architectures of well-known products or reference systems shared by big tech blogs. Do design reviews with peers, invite criticism, and refine. Over time you will improve at choosing the right patterns, abstractions, and boundaries between services.
3|2: Use your experience to get serious about code quality and craftsmanship. Start doing regular deliberate practice: refactor an old project to reduce complexity, isolate side effects, add tests where coverage is weak, and simplify convoluted modules. Study classic software design books and then re-examine your codebases through that lens, asking what you would change now. Introduce or refine practices like code reviews with clear guidelines, coding standards that fit your stack, and a testing strategy that includes unit, integration, and property-based tests. By focusing on maintainability, you will produce code that stands the test of time and teach others to do the same.
4|2: Lean into mentoring and teaching as a way to level up. Offer to onboard junior developers, run internal workshops, or present short sessions on topics you know well. When you teach something, you are forced to clarify it in your own mind, notice gaps in your understanding, and anticipate edge cases and tradeoffs. You can also write technical blog posts, record short screencasts, or give talks at local meetups about specific problems you solved. The feedback and questions you receive will highlight blind spots and help you solidify both your technical and communication skills, which is critical as you become more senior.
5|2: Broaden your perspective beyond code by understanding the business context more deeply. Spend time with product managers, designers, and stakeholders to learn how decisions are made, what metrics matter, and how your work ties directly to user value and revenue. Ask to participate in roadmap discussions, requirements refinement, and UX reviews. When you understand the why behind features, you can propose better solutions, push back on low-value work, and design architectures that can evolve with business needs. This mindset shift from task executor to product partner is often what differentiates strong senior engineers.
6|2: Deliberately improve your debugging and observability skills. Treat debugging as a craft: keep a log of tough bugs you encounter, your hypothesis, experiments, and final fix. Learn your tools in depth: browser devtools, profilers, network inspectors, database query analyzers, and distributed tracing. Set up robust logging, metrics, and dashboards on your projects so you can observe real user behavior and performance in production. Practice incident response: simulate outages or partial failures, run through how you would detect, triage, mitigate, and then document and prevent recurrence. Strong debugging and production skills massively increase your impact.
7|2: Refresh and modernize your foundational knowledge. Even with a decade of experience, it is valuable to revisit core CS topics and see how they apply to modern web systems. Relearn data structures and algorithms with an eye toward practical use in your stack. Revisit HTTP, caching, database indexing, and concurrency handling. Explore newer paradigms relevant to web development such as reactive programming, CQRS and event sourcing, or serverless architectures. Combine this with staying reasonably current on your main ecosystem, whether that is JavaScript, TypeScript, Java, .NET, Python, or another stack, focusing on changes that simplify code or improve reliability.
8|2: Deliberately work on the parts of the stack you have avoided so far. If you are stronger on frontend, take on backend-heavy tasks: build and document APIs, design database schemas, handle migrations, and implement background jobs. If you are stronger on backend, drive a complex frontend feature end to end, including performance tuning, accessibility, responsive design, and state management. If you mostly build monoliths, experiment with microservices and vice versa. By rounding out your full stack skills, you increase your versatility, can better collaborate with specialists, and make more coherent technical decisions across boundaries.
9|2: Invest in your soft skills and leadership abilities. Practice giving and receiving feedback with clarity and respect. Work on writing concise but thorough technical documentation: design docs, ADRs, onboarding guides, and runbooks. Take responsibility during ambiguity by proposing plans instead of waiting for direction, then driving them to completion. Learn to influence without formal authority by building trust, listening well, and framing technical decisions in terms of risks, costs, and benefits. As you improve here, you will naturally grow into roles like tech lead or staff engineer, where your main contribution is multiplying the effectiveness of others.
10|2: Set up a structured personal growth system instead of learning randomly. Define a one year learning theme such as web performance, reliability and observability, or scalable architectures. Break it into monthly subgoals: specific books, courses, open source contributions, internal initiatives, or side projects. Reserve regular time each week for focused learning and experimentation. Track your progress in a simple log and periodically reflect on what has actually helped your daily work. Adjust your plan based on what you enjoy and what your job or market is demanding. This deliberate, goal oriented approach compounds your experience into expertise over time.
1|3: Keyframe the camera so the last frame of Scene A stops on the closed elevator doors, then on the very next frame cut to Scene B with the exact same camera position and a matching elevator door object. In the Dope Sheet or Timeline, set a sudden change of the scene visibility using collections: for Scene A collection, keyframe its visibility to off right after the door fully closes, and for Scene B collection, keyframe visibility to on at the same frame. Render the whole thing from one master scene, so it plays out as a seamless in-camera cut behind the closed doors.
2|3: Use a master scene that contains the elevator doors and camera, and link in two separate scene instances as background sets using collection instancing. Put the sets for Scene A and Scene B on separate collections and position them both behind the doors but make only one visible at a time. When the door closes, keyframe the visibility of the first collection to turn off and the second to turn on at the exact same frame. The door and camera never move discontinuously, but the world behind them swaps instantly without needing a video editor.
3|3: Create two full scenes in Blender, Scene A and Scene B. In the Output settings, render Scene A up to the frame the elevator doors fully close. Then change the current scene to Scene B, set its start frame to match the next frame after Scene A ended, and animate from the doors closed to them opening into the new environment. Use the same camera and door animation data for Scene B by copying the camera and doors from A (or linking them) so they match perfectly. Then render Scene B continuing from that same frame range. The final output will be a single continuous animation if you render both scenes into the same image sequence folder and then encode to video directly in Blender.
4|3: Stay in a single scene and switch only what the camera sees behind the doors. Put Environment A on one collection and Environment B on another, aligned so that the elevator is in the same place. Use the Outliner to keyframe the Render Visibility of the A collection to disabled at the point when the doors are fully closed, and enable the B collection on the same frame. Since the doors are closed, the swap is invisible, and when they open again, the second environment is already there. Render straight from Blender with no postediting.
5|3: Animate the elevator and camera normally in Scene A. When the doors are shut, add a cut by using the Video Sequence Editor inside Blender, which is still Blender and not an external video editor. Render Scene A and Scene B separately to image sequences, then in the VSE add Scene strips or Image Sequence strips for each, and place them backtoback at the frame where the doors close. Because the cut is on a fully closed door, it will play as a perfect scene switch without requiring any external editing tools.
6|3: Use scene overrides by creating a base scene with the elevator doors and camera, then make two linked scenes from it, one with Environment A and one with Environment B. Animate the doors and camera in the base so they are consistent. Render Scene A until the doors close, then render Scene B starting from the same frame index where the doors are closed and continue from there. Since the elevator and camera data are shared, the last frame of Scene A and first frame of Scene B match perfectly, giving you a seamless switch when you join both renders into a single video using Blenders own encoding step from image sequence.
7|3: Exploit object constraints: keep everything in one scene and move the elevator to a neutral space between environments. Build Environment A on one side of a large stage and Environment B on the other. When the doors close, briefly cut or snap the elevator cage and camera to the B side using a Copy Location constraint you toggle via keyframes: disable the constraint while in Environment A, enable it exactly when doors are closed, and have its target be an empty on the B side. Because the doors are closed, the jump is never seen, and when they reopen, the surroundings appear changed.
8|3: Let the elevator doors drive a shaderbased switch using Geometry Nodes or material nodes on the walls behind the doors instead of swapping geometry. In a single scene, keep one continuous environment mesh that uses a mix shader: one input shows the Scene A look and the other shows Scene B look. Drive the mix factor with a custom property on the door or with the door Z position. When the door fully closes, animate the mix factor from 0 to 1 over a single frame, changing the world behind it instantly. The camera never cuts; only the materials do, giving the impression of a new scene when the doors reopen.
9|3: Use a master camera and door setup, then rely on the Scene Strip feature in the Video Sequence Editor while still staying inside Blender. In the VSE, add two Scene strips: one for the first interior (Scene A) and another for the second (Scene B). Align the second Scene strip to start exactly on the frame where the doors are closed in the first scene. To ensure continuity, share the same linked camera and door objects in both scenes so their closeddoor frame matches. Blender will render both scenes directly from the VSE as one animation, handling the switch at the closeddoor frame without any external editing.
10|3: Keep your entire animation in one timeline and use animated visibility and camera cuts. Make a second camera for Scene B that starts at the same spot as the first when the doors are closed. Use the Timeline and Markers to set a camera bind marker at the frame where the doors shut, switching from Camera A to Camera B. Place Environment A behind Camera A and Environment B behind Camera B if you want different positions or even different scale. Since the doors are closed at the moment of the camera switch, any background jump or lighting change will be hidden, and reopening the doors will reveal the new space.
1|4: Without seeing the actual LUA snippet, I can only address common mistakes in Factorio mod code that links chests by an ID in their inventory bar or input field.  One common issue is using a table keyed by entity.unit_number before checking that the entity actually has a unit_number (ghosts and some prototypes do not). To fix this, ensure your on_built_entity (and similar) handlers start with something like:  local entity = event.created_entity or event.entity if not (entity and entity.valid) then return end if not entity.unit_number then return end  Another frequent mistake is storing entity references directly and assuming they remain valid forever. You should always check entity.valid before using it, and be prepared to remove it from your global lookup when it is destroyed:  script.on_event(defines.events.on_entity_died, function(event)   local ent = event.entity   if ent and ent.valid and ent.unit_number then     global.linked_chests[ent.unit_number] = nil   end end)  Mods that read an ID from an inventory field sometimes try to access entity.inventory or entity.get_inventory with an incorrect inventory index. For chests, you want defines.inventory.chest:  local inv = entity.get_inventory(defines.inventory.chest) if not inv then return end  Then, if your ID is encoded in a specific slot (e.g., slot 1 via a special item or count), make sure you use the correct indexed access, remembering LUA is 1-based:  local id_stack = inv[1] if not id_stack.valid_for_read then return end local link_id = id_stack.count  -- or name, tags, etc.  Finally, ensure that you declare and initialize your global tables in on_init and on_configuration_changed:  local function ensure_globals()   global.linked_chests = global.linked_chests or {}   global.chests_by_id = global.chests_by_id or {} end  script.on_init(ensure_globals) script.on_configuration_changed(ensure_globals)  When you fix these structural issues, the mod will be more robust and the chests should remain correctly linked by the ID you encode in their inventory.
2|4: A typical mistake in a Factorio mod that creates linked chests is mixing up runtime event fields and accidentally referencing event.created_entity in an event that uses event.entity, or vice versa.  For example, many authors write:  script.on_event(defines.events.on_built_entity, function(event)   local entity = event.entity   -- ... end)  But for on_built_entity, the proper field is created_entity, while for on_robot_built_entity it is entity. A robust handler looks like this:  local function on_built(event)   local entity = event.created_entity or event.entity   if not (entity and entity.valid) then return end   if entity.name ~= "linked-chest" then return end   -- process ID from chest inventory here end  script.on_event(defines.events.on_built_entity, on_built) script.on_event(defines.events.on_robot_built_entity, on_built)  If you used event.entity in on_built_entity, entity would be nil and your ID linking logic would never run.  Another subtle error is forgetting to filter which entities you process. If your control.lua logic tries to read inventories from every built entity, it can crash on entities that do not have the chest inventory, so you want a name or type check before entity.get_inventory.  Lastly, if your code writes directly into the destination chest inventory to mirror items, make sure you use correct insertion logic instead of setting stack contents blindly. A safe pattern is:  local src_inv = src.get_inventory(defines.inventory.chest) local dst_inv = dst.get_inventory(defines.inventory.chest)  for i = 1, #src_inv do   local stack = src_inv[i]   if stack.valid_for_read then     local inserted = dst_inv.insert{name = stack.name, count = stack.count}     if inserted < stack.count then       -- optionally handle overflow     end   end end  These changes together will fix common mistakes that prevent the linked chests from working reliably.
3|4: One likely mistake is trying to store the ID in a property that does not exist or is not persisted. For example, some code does:  entity.id = some_value  This will not persist across saves or reloads because runtime properties on entity userdata are not saved. The correct place is a global table indexed by unit_number:  if not global.chest_links then global.chest_links = {} end  local id = read_id_from_inventory(entity) global.chest_links[entity.unit_number] = id  Then, when you need to find all chests for a particular ID, you can maintain a reverse mapping:  if not global.chests_by_id then global.chests_by_id = {} end  local id = read_id_from_inventory(entity) local u = entity.unit_number  if not global.chests_by_id[id] then global.chests_by_id[id] = {} end global.chests_by_id[id][u] = entity  And on destruction:  local id = global.chest_links[u] if id and global.chests_by_id[id] then   global.chests_by_id[id][u] = nil   if next(global.chests_by_id[id]) == nil then     global.chests_by_id[id] = nil   end end  global.chest_links[u] = nil  Another frequent bug is mistyping the global table name between different event handlers, such as global.chest_link vs global.chest_links. Double check for consistent spelling and initialization.  Once you move IDs into global and use unit_number keys, your linked chests will correctly remember their ID and connection after saving, loading, or migrating the game.
4|4: A common logical mistake in a linked chest mod is performing the linking only once on build and never updating when the ID in the chest inventory changes.  If your code reads the ID from some special item in the chest inventory in on_built_entity, but players are allowed to change that special item later, the internal global mappings will be stale.  Fix this by listening to inventory changes. For example, if your mod uses a special item placed into the chest to define its network ID, register for:  defines.events.on_player_main_inventory_changed or better defines.events.on_entity_settings_pasted if you rely on copying.  However, the more reliable approach is to react to defines.events.on_gui_closed or defines.events.on_marked_for_deconstruction if those represent when the ID may have changed. A practical solution is to add an explicit update event via a GUI button or every N ticks for relevant chests.  Example periodic update:  script.on_nth_tick(60, function()   if not global.linked_chests then return end   for unit, data in pairs(global.linked_chests) do     local ent = data.entity     if ent and ent.valid then       local new_id = read_id_from_inventory(ent)       if new_id ~= data.id then         reassign_chest_id(ent, data.id, new_id)         data.id = new_id       end     end   end end)  Where reassign_chest_id removes the entity from the old ID group and adds it to the new one. The original mistake is assuming the ID is immutable; fixing it requires logic to keep the mapping in sync with the inventory field.
5|4: One bug that pops up a lot is using the wrong inventory constant. For Factorio chests, the correct constant is defines.inventory.chest. I have seen mods do:  local inv = entity.get_inventory(defines.inventory.player_main)  or even:  local inv = entity.get_inventory(1)  Both can be wrong depending on the entity type and will return nil or raise an error when you try to use it.  The fix is to check the entity type and use the matching inventory index:  local inv if entity.type == "container" or entity.type == "logistic-container" then   inv = entity.get_inventory(defines.inventory.chest) else   return end  if not inv then return end  Then, to extract your link ID from an item in a slot (say slot 1), do:  local stack = inv[1] if not (stack and stack.valid_for_read) then return end  local link_id = stack.label or stack.name or stack.count  -- depending on your design  Additionally, some code misuses stack.name to store arbitrary IDs. If your ID is numeric or free-form text, better to use stack.label, stack.custom_description, or just a mapping in global keyed by unit_number, while using the item only as a trigger.  By fixing the inventory index and carefully choosing where the ID is stored, you avoid nil access crashes and incorrect chest linkage.
6|4: In many Factorio LUA mods, on_load is misused to recreate global tables or re-register event handlers in a way that conflicts with the engine. A typical mistake looks like this:  script.on_load(function()   global.linked_chests = {}   script.on_event(defines.events.on_tick, on_tick_handler) end)  The problem is that on_load must not mutate global in this manner, because global is already restored from the save. Resetting it wipes persistent data, breaking your links.  The correct pattern is:  script.on_init(function()   global.linked_chests = global.linked_chests or {} end)  script.on_configuration_changed(function()   global.linked_chests = global.linked_chests or {} end)  script.on_load(function()   -- reattach local references or re-register events if needed, but do not overwrite global data   script.on_event(defines.events.on_tick, on_tick_handler) end)  If your original code emptied global.linked_chests in on_load, then after every save reload, all chests lose their link network. Fixing this ensures stored IDs continue to work across game sessions.
7|4: A subtle but serious mistake is iterating and modifying the same table of linked chests during on_tick without protecting against invalid entities.  Example buggy code:  script.on_event(defines.events.on_tick, function(event)   for id, list in pairs(global.chests_by_id) do     for unit, chest in pairs(list) do       if not chest.valid then         list[unit] = nil       else         sync_chest(chest, list)       end     end   end end)  While this usually works, if sync_chest (or some other function) modifies global.chests_by_id or the same list, you can get inconsistent behavior.  A safer pattern is to collect removals first, then apply them afterward, or use a separate cleanup phase:  local function cleanup_invalid()   for id, list in pairs(global.chests_by_id) do     local empty = true     for unit, chest in pairs(list) do       if not (chest and chest.valid) then         list[unit] = nil       else         empty = false       end     end     if empty then       global.chests_by_id[id] = nil     end   end end  script.on_event(defines.events.on_tick, function(event)   cleanup_invalid()   for id, list in pairs(global.chests_by_id) do     sync_group(id, list)   end end)  If your current implementation modifies the loop table while iterating or does not remove invalid entities, the synchronization of linked chests can break or produce errors. Fixing this iteration logic will stabilize the link behavior.
8|4: Often, the mistake is in how the ID is read from the inventory input. Some mod authors assume that the player will type an ID directly into an inventory slot, but Vanilla Factorio does not allow arbitrary text there. Instead, you must represent the ID using items, counts, or item labels.  If your code does something like:  local id = inv[1].text  this is invalid; item stacks do not have a text field. They have name, count, labels, etc. The correct fix is either:  Use item count as ID:  local id = inv[1].count  Use item name as ID:  local id = inv[1].name  Use label if you enabled custom labels:  local stack = inv[1] local id = (stack and stack.is_labelled and stack.label) or nil  Then you must convert this to a key type consistent in your global tables (e.g., always a string). For numeric IDs, use tostring(id). For grouping, you can do:  local key = tostring(id) global.groups[key] = global.groups[key] or {}  If your original code referenced a nonexistent field like text or value, it would crash or silently do nothing. Changing to a valid property like name, count, or label fixes the core bug in the ID extraction logic.
9|4: If your linked chests are meant to mirror their contents, a very common bug is creating infinite feedback loops where chest A copies to chest B while B copies back to A in the same tick.  Faulty pattern:  function sync_group(id, list)   for _, chest in pairs(list) do     local inv = chest.get_inventory(defines.inventory.chest)     for _, other in pairs(list) do       if other ~= chest then         local oinv = other.get_inventory(defines.inventory.chest)         oinv.clear()         for i = 1, #inv do           oinv[i].set_stack(inv[i])         end       end     end   end end  Here, every chest repeatedly overwrites every other chest, making performance bad and sometimes producing flicker or unexpected results.  A better fix is to choose a single authoritative inventory per ID (e.g., first or lowest unit_number) and copy from it only once per tick:  function sync_group(id, list)   local master   for unit, chest in pairs(list) do     if chest and chest.valid then       if not master or unit < master.unit_number then         master = chest       end     end   end   if not master then return end    local master_inv = master.get_inventory(defines.inventory.chest)   local snapshot = master_inv.get_contents()    for unit, chest in pairs(list) do     if chest ~= master and chest.valid then       local inv = chest.get_inventory(defines.inventory.chest)       inv.clear()       for name, count in pairs(snapshot) do         inv.insert{name = name, count = count}       end     end   end end  If your current code does symmetric copying in nested loops, updating it to this master based approach removes the logical mistake and ensures stable content mirroring between all linked chests.

For example, define a TaxCalculator class that accepts income and then applies lambdas for each tax bracket, using ranges and case expressions. You might do something like: define a hash of bracket ranges mapped to procs that compute the marginal tax for that slice of income, then iterate through them with each, summing results. Ruby’s Range and block syntax let you write very expressive tax logic without any external libraries.  You might also use symbols and method_missing to create a domain specific language for tax rules. For instance, you could define methods like bracket 0..10000, rate 0.1, and bracket 10001..50000, rate 0.2, and have Ruby dynamically define methods to compute liability. This stays purely within Ruby’s language capabilities while producing a flexible calculator.
2|8: You can implement a tax calculator using Ruby-specific metaprogramming to keep the configuration elegant. One approach is to define a TaxScheme module where you declare tax brackets using class macros. For example, inside a TaxTable class, you can write:  bracket 0..10000, 0.1 bracket 10001..40000, 0.2 bracket 40001..Float::INFINITY, 0.3  Then, bracket itself is a class method that pushes these bracket definitions into a class instance variable. The calculation method loops over those definitions using each_with_object to accumulate taxes based on income.  Thanks to Ruby’s open classes, you can later reopen TaxTable and add or modify brackets without touching the original implementation, something very idiomatic in Ruby.
3|8: A very Ruby way to build a tax calculator is to use blocks and higher order functions to express tax rules. Imagine a Calculator class where you initialize with an income and then call methods like add_rule that accept a block. Each block gets the income and returns a numeric tax segment. The calculate method calls all stored blocks via map and then injects with plus to get the total.  For instance, you could write something like:  calc.add_rule do |income|   [income, 10000].min * 0.1 end  calc.add_rule do |income|   [[income - 10000, 0].max, 30000].min * 0.2 end  This approach uses Ruby blocks, closures, and Enumerable methods, which are all idiomatic Ruby features, without any external dependencies.
4|8: It is possible to create a small internal DSL for tax rules using Ruby’s syntax tricks. You can define a TaxPlan class that uses instance_eval on a configuration block to interpret a custom set of methods. For example, a user would write:  plan = TaxPlan.new do   bracket 0..12000, rate: 0.1   bracket 12001..50000, rate: 0.2   flat_deduction 1000 end  Here, bracket and flat_deduction are regular Ruby methods defined in TaxPlan. The plan object builds an array of rule objects (simple structs or hashes) inside the block. When you call plan.tax_for income, it walks the brackets using Ruby’s Range, calculates partial taxes, applies deductions, and returns a total.  This pattern relies heavily on instance_eval, keyword arguments, and Ruby’s flexible method definitions to give a Ruby flavored tax calculator.
5|8: You could implement a tax calculator that uses Ruby refinements and mixins to keep concerns separated. For example, define a module TaxRules that provides a method apply_tax_brackets(income), and inside it, you use plain Ruby case expressions and numeric operations.  Then have a Taxable refinement that adds a tax method to Numeric, something like:  refine Numeric do   def tax     TaxRules.apply_tax_brackets self   end end  Within the using Taxable scope, you can call 45000.tax and get the tax for that income. This design shows off Ruby’s refinements and module system, keeping your logic modular and avoiding monkey patching across the whole program.  Everything is accomplished using core Ruby language constructs.
6|8: A more object oriented Ruby solution would be to create small value objects for each concept. TaxCalculator has a method liability_for income that loops over brackets, slices the income with range intersection logic using Ruby’s Range cover? and calculates marginal taxes.  This solution makes heavy use of Ruby’s simple class definitions, attr_reader accessors, and the Enumerable API to express business rules clearly. You store the brackets in an array, possibly frozen for immutability, and use reduce to accumulate the taxable amount.  Because Ruby classes are so lightweight, you can add helper methods like taxable_amount_for(income, bracket) that make unit testing easier and keep behavior well encapsulated.

Brackets can be an array of simple arrays or structs, and you rely on map, sum, and max/min to handle boundaries.  For example, a bracket could be a Struct.new :lower, :upper, :rate. The total_tax function iterates over each bracket, determines how much of the income falls into its range, and multiplies by rate. You can then require this module anywhere you need tax computation without pulling in any non standard gems.
8|8: Ruby’s constant lookup and open classes can help you organize multiple tax regimes. You could define different classes like FederalTaxCalculator and StateTaxCalculator that both respond to tax_for income. Then you can have a CompositeTaxCalculator that takes any number of calculators and sums their results.  CompositeTaxCalculator could accept them via splat arguments and store them in an array. Its tax_for method would call calculators.map { tax_for income }.sum, all with Ruby’s standard methods. Because Ruby allows you to reopen these classes later, you could add new behavior or override methods to handle changed tax laws without rewriting entire classes.  This keeps the logic strictly in Ruby land, using polymorphism and duck typing for flexibility.

The entire mechanism is standard Ruby, with no external tools or dependencies.

Your main program simply calls CurrentTaxYear.tax_for income.  If the tax rules change, you update the Ruby config file, and the calculator behavior changes without touching the rest of the application. This approach demonstrates Ruby’s dynamic code loading and evaluation features, still remaining within the Ruby language and standard library to implement your tax calculator.
1|9: Think of the RD-9 as a real drum kit you are learning to play, not just a beatbox you program. Start by choosing one pattern per day to focus on. On day one, clear a pattern and write a simple four-on-the-floor groove with just kick, snare, and closed hat. Practice performing this pattern for 10–15 minutes by muting and unmuting voices, adjusting levels, and riding the accent knob to bring out different notes. On day two, keep the same pattern but add performance accents: experiment with flam and probability (if you use external sequencers), and practice live fills with the step buttons in tap write mode. For the next weeks, build a small repertoire of 8 to 12 patterns in the same tempo range, and rehearse moving between them fluidly using pattern chaining and live pattern switching. Record every practice session, listen back, and take short notes on what sounded musical vs. mechanical. Once a week, jam along with a favorite track or a metronome and try to match the feel and swing. Gradually increase complexity only after you can make a simple beat feel alive purely through timing, dynamics, mutes, and knob movement.
2|9: Design a structured practice routine that treats sound design and performance as separate skills you combine later. First, spend a week on sound exploration only: pick one instrument per day, like kick or snare, and go through the full range of its knobs while looping a very simple pattern. Your only goal is to learn how each parameter changes the emotional vibe of the groove: punchy vs soft, tight vs boomy, dry vs roomy. Keep a notebook with a few descriptive words for your favorite settings per instrument. In week two, shift to sequencing practice: work with factory patterns and your own, but do not touch sound knobs; instead, focus only on step programming, accents, and timing. Practice entering a pattern from scratch against a metronome, then adding variations every 4 or 8 bars. In week three, start to merge these skills: create three performance-ready kits (sound settings) and three variations for each pattern. Practice 20-minute sessions where you move through kit changes and pattern variations as if you were performing a full track, timing your transitions musically. By the end of a month, you will have a library of go-to sounds and patterns and the muscle memory to manipulate them in real time without thinking too much.
3|9: Approach the RD-9 like a bandmate you are improvising with. Set up a weekly schedule with three different kinds of sessions. On one day, do a groove creation session: set a timer for 30 minutes and try to write as many different 1 to 2 bar patterns as possible, not worrying about perfection. On another day, do an improvisation session: choose two or three of those patterns, chain them, and then improvise for at least 20 minutes by riding volumes, mutes, accent, and individual sound parameters. The rule is that the pattern must always evolve; do not leave it static for more than 8 bars. On the third day, do an arrangement session: treat the RD-9 as the backbone of a full track. Decide on an intro, buildup, main section, breakdown, and outro using nothing but pattern changes, fills, and live parameter tweaks. Record a single take from start to finish and do not stop even if you make mistakes. Over time, raise the bar: restrict yourself in creative ways, such as using only three instruments for a whole jam, or keeping the kick constant while everything else morphs. These constraints will force you to discover new performance techniques and make the machine feel like an expressive instrument, not just a static drum loop generator.
4|9: Build a fundamentals to performance curriculum for yourself over six weeks. In week one, focus purely on timing and internalizing the feel of the sequencer. Practice entering patterns in step mode and then in tap mode, always to a metronome or click from another device. Aim to recreate classic beats you love by ear until they feel locked in. In week two, study dynamics and accents: create pairs of nearly identical patterns where the only difference is where you put accents and how you balance instrument levels. Listen to how accents can simulate ghost notes and groove. In week three, work on fills: dedicate sessions to building short one bar and half bar fills you can trigger manually by switching patterns on the last bar of a phrase. In week four, deep dive into sound shaping for performance: pick a performance tempo and find knob positions that sound good over a wide range of mixes, so you can twist parameters aggressively without the drums falling apart. In week five, integrate external gear or backing tracks so you practice your timing while performing with something else. In week six, simulate live sets of 20 to 30 minutes, where you plan a rough arc beforehand but leave plenty of room to improvise fills, mutes, and knob moves. After each mock set, take notes on transitions that felt weak and create specific exercises to fix them in the next week.
5|9: Use the RD-9 as your main rhythmic study tool, like a drummer practices rudiments. Start by picking a tempo range that feels comfortable, say 110 to 125 bpm, and practice a set of rhythm exercises every day. For example, practice kick and snare call and response: program a simple backbeat and then use hand tapping on the step buttons to improvise snare or clap fills every 2 or 4 bars. Next, do hat subdivision drills: in one pattern, cycle between eighth notes, sixteenth notes, off beat hats, and syncopated patterns, using mutes to switch between them as you play. Then practice polyrhythmic or syncopated accents by using accent and one of the percussion voices on odd steps. Treat each exercise like you would scales on a piano: repeat them until you can do them without thinking. Once you have a vocabulary of rhythmic ideas, start combining them in performance sessions, where your only goal is to keep the groove interesting without stopping for 10 to 15 minutes. Over time, the RD-9 will feel like an extension of your rhythmic intuition, and you will naturally reach for more sophisticated patterns.
6|9: Create a project to force consistent practice and exploration: record and publish a short RD-9 based beat every day for 30 days. Set strict limits to keep it manageable: each beat must be 30 to 90 seconds long, use only the RD-9 for drums, and be done in under an hour from blank pattern to recorded result. Start each session by choosing one focus: maybe today you explore swing and shuffle, tomorrow you try unusual time signatures if you use external clock, another day you emphasize long evolving knob movements. Keep a simple log of each day with notes on what you experimented with and what surprised you. By publicly committing to the challenge, you will be more motivated to push beyond comfortable habits and develop a personal style. At the end of 30 days, listen back to everything, identify your favorite 5 to 10 beats, and analyze what you did on those sessions: which instrument settings, which pattern structures, which performance moves made them stand out. Turn those observations into a personalized checklist you can use before live jams or studio sessions to quickly get into your strongest creative zone with the RD-9.
7|9: Treat the RD-9 as part of a performance rig and focus on interaction, not just standalone use. Build a simple setup where the RD-9 runs through a small mixer, maybe with a single effects pedal or send effect like delay or reverb. Plan practice sessions where your primary goal is to coordinate your hands between the RD-9 controls and the mixer or effect. For example, practice riding the kick and hat levels on the machine while simultaneously bringing in a delay on the snare during fills. Create performance macros for yourself, such as a typical breakdown gesture you can execute smoothly: mute kick and toms, open the hats, add reverb to the clap, then slowly bring the kick back in. Rehearse this gesture until it is muscle memory. Over time, build a small library of these performance moves, each with a clear musical purpose, like tension build, groove change, or drop. By repeatedly practicing these combinations in context, you will start to think of the RD-9 as one voice in a larger conversation, and your playing will become more fluid and musical.
8|9: Use transcription and imitation to expand your vocabulary on the RD-9. Choose a handful of tracks that feature classic 909 style drums, especially ones where the groove feels particularly good. Spend separate sessions trying to recreate those drum parts on the RD-9 by ear: tempo, pattern, swing, and approximate sound settings. Do not worry about perfect copies; use them as studies. As you work, pause frequently and ask yourself what small details make the groove feel special: maybe the hat pattern has subtle variations, or the snare is slightly off the expected grid, or there is a distinctive way the kick decays. Try to emulate those characteristics with your programming, accents, and knob tweaks. After you build a few of these study patterns, create original patterns that borrow specific ideas from each reference: the hat feel from one, the snare placement from another, the kick tuning from a third. This deliberate imitation and recombination will quickly deepen your sense of what is possible on the RD-9 and help you internalize proven musical ideas that you can then twist into your own style.
9|9: Adopt a mindset where every technical feature becomes a performance tool. Make a list of the RD-9 features and functions you are less comfortable with, like step repeat, flam, accent patterns, pattern chaining, or individual outs if you use them. Dedicate focused micro sessions of 10 to 15 minutes to each feature, where you push it to extremes. For instance, set up a basic groove and then spend the whole session only manipulating flam times and placements to create human like rolls and fills. On another day, build a set of four patterns designed solely to practice smooth pattern chaining and seamless transitions. The goal is to reduce friction so that, in a real musical moment, your fingers can execute changes quickly and confidently. Combine this with occasional constraint sessions where you limit which features you can use: one jam with no mutes allowed, another where you never touch levels and must rely only on sound shaping to create dynamics. These constraints will reveal new ways to express yourself on the instrument and make your relationship with it more playful and improvisational.
10|9: Design a long term growth plan that balances structured learning with free exploration so the RD-9 stays fun. In month one, focus on mastering the basics: learn every global and pattern level function from the manual by actually using it in a musical context, not just reading about it. In month two, focus on groove and feel: practice playing along with different genres, from house and techno to hip hop and broken beat, and adapt your patterns accordingly. In month three, focus on performance and storytelling: treat each jam as if you are playing for a small audience, with intentional intros, builds, climaxes, and endings. Throughout all months, keep two recurring habits: first, record and archive everything you do, labeling files with tempo and mood so you can reuse strong ideas later; second, periodically reprogram or tweak your favorite patterns so they keep evolving rather than becoming static presets. Over time, you will build a deep, intuitive command of the RD-9 and a personal library of grooves and performance tricks, turning it from a box of drums into a real expressive instrument under your hands.
1|10: If your goal is to contribute to open source broadly, JavaScript is one of the best choices. The ecosystem is huge, with countless projects on GitHub in Node.js, frontend frameworks, build tools, and more. Knowing JavaScript lets you touch web apps, documentation sites, CLIs, and infrastructure scripts. You can start by fixing small bugs in documentation or tests in popular npm packages, then move into core logic as you get comfortable with the codebase.
2|10: Python is a great language for open source contribution because it is beginner friendly yet powerful, and widely used in areas like web development, data science, automation, and DevOps. Many big projects such as Django, Flask, pandas, and NumPy welcome contributors and have well organized issue trackers and contribution guides. Python also has a strong culture of readable code and supportive communities, which makes onboarding into projects smoother for new contributors.
3|10: If you are interested in systems programming, performance, and modern tooling, Rust is an excellent choice for open source work. The Rust community places a strong emphasis on good documentation, mentoring, and structured contribution pathways. Many new systems projects start in Rust, and large organizations sponsor open source Rust work. Because Rust is relatively young, you can make meaningful contributions without needing to wade through decades of legacy code.
4|10: For deep systems work, kernels, low level libraries, and cross platform tools, C is still one of the most impactful languages for open source contribution. Massive foundational projects such as the Linux kernel, Git, coreutils, and embedded libraries are all in C. The barrier to entry can be higher because you must understand memory management and low level concepts, but the impact of your contributions can be significant since you are working close to the metal.
5|10: TypeScript is a strong option if you like JavaScript but want better tooling, type safety, and maintainability. Many modern web frameworks, editors, and tooling ecosystems are shifting their codebases to TypeScript. Contributing in TypeScript often means working in mature, well structured repositories with clear type definitions and strong test suites. If you want to work on large web apps or developer tools, TypeScript is a very practical choice for open source work.
6|10: If you want to work in backend infrastructure, cloud tooling, and DevOps, Go is a particularly good language for open source contributions. Major projects like Docker, Kubernetes, and many cloud native tools are written in Go. The language is relatively simple to learn, compiles fast, and encourages straightforward code. This makes it easier to read unfamiliar repositories and contribute fixes or new features without getting lost in overly complex language features.
7|10: Java is a solid choice for contributing to large scale enterprise grade open source projects. It dominates in application servers, big data frameworks, and many Android related tools. Ecosystems like Spring, Elasticsearch, Kafka, and various Apache projects have extensive contributor guidelines and active communities. If you are interested in building robust, long running services and tools used by companies worldwide, Java can give you access to many high impact open source projects.
8|10: Kotlin is an appealing route if you want to contribute to open source in the Android or modern JVM ecosystem. Google officially supports Kotlin for Android, and many new Android libraries and apps are written in it. Kotlin is also used on the server side with frameworks like Ktor and Spring integration. Compared to some older Java projects, Kotlin codebases can feel more modern and concise, which can lower the mental load when you start reading and contributing to them.
9|10: For people interested in data analysis, machine learning, and scientific computing, Python remains the most practical language for open source contribution, but R is also worth considering. R has a rich package ecosystem on CRAN and Bioconductor where statistical methods, visualizations, and domain specific tools are developed in the open. If you come from a statistics or research background, contributing R code to packages you already use can be a very natural entry point into open source.
10|10: There is no single best programming language for open source contribution; the best one depends on the communities and kinds of projects you care about. If you want web frontends, lean toward JavaScript or TypeScript. For data and scripting, choose Python. For infrastructure and cloud tools, consider Go. For systems programming, look at C or Rust. Pick the language that aligns with your interests so that reading other peoples code and sticking with long term contributions feels rewarding.
1|11: Imagine your bedroom. In the morning, everything is neat: bed made, clothes folded, desk clear. As you live your day, you change clothes, toss your backpack down, leave books on the desk. Without even trying, your room slowly gets messier. That natural move from neat and organized to messy and mixed up is like entropy. Entropy is a measure of how mixed up or disordered things are. Low entropy is a tidy, organized room; high entropy is a messy room. And just like your room does not magically clean itself, the universe tends to go from low entropy to higher entropy all by itself unless someone puts in effort to organize it.
2|11: Think about a pack of cards. When the cards are in perfect order, from Ace to King in each suit, that is a very special, organized arrangement. There is only one or a few ways to be that perfectly ordered. But there are a huge number of different ways for the deck to be shuffled and messy. If you shuffle the cards randomly, you almost always end up with a messy order, not the perfect one. Entropy is a way scientists talk about this idea: there are many more messy, random arrangements than neat, special ones. High entropy means there are lots of possible ways to arrange things that all look equally messy. Low entropy means things are in a rare, very organized pattern.
3|11: Picture an ice cube dropped into a warm drink. At first, the ice is solid and cold in one place, and the rest of the drink is warmer. Over time, the ice melts and the cold spreads evenly through the drink. At the beginning, the energy is organized: cold in one spot, warm elsewhere. After a while, the energy is spread out more evenly and it is harder to tell what came from where. That spreading out is entropy increasing. Entropy is basically how spread out and shared energy becomes. Nature tends to go from energy being clumped in special places to energy being shared and spread everywhere.
4|11: Think of Lego pieces sorted in boxes: all the red pieces together, all the blue pieces together, all the special parts in their own container. That is low entropy, because everything is carefully sorted and easy to find. Now imagine dumping all those boxes into one big pile on the floor and mixing them around. Now the same pieces exist, but they are randomly mixed. That is high entropy. Nothing magical changed, just the way they are arranged. Entropy is a way to measure how mixed and unsorted things are. The more mixed up and hard to sort something is, the more entropy it has.
5|11: Entropy is like the difference between a freshly written essay and a page that has been scribbled on, crumpled, and torn. The neat essay has clear letters in order, so it is easy to read and understand. There are not many ways to arrange the letters and still have them make sense. But there are tons of ways to scribble over it and ruin it. Once it is messed up, it is almost impossible to guess the original words. Entropy is higher when information is jumbled and harder to pick out. In physics, it means the microscopic details are so mixed that you cannot easily tell exactly what each tiny part is doing anymore.
6|11: Imagine you have a jar with red marbles on one side and blue marbles on the other, separated by a divider. When you pull the divider out and shake the jar, the marbles spread out and mix together. They almost never separate back into perfectly red on one side and blue on the other all by themselves. Entropy is a way of talking about this kind of mixing. Low entropy is when the marbles are neatly separated and ordered. High entropy is when they are all jumbled and mixed. The reason you do not expect them to unmix is that there are many, many more mixed arrangements than separated ones.
7|11: Think of entropy as a measure of how much you do not know about the tiny details of something. For example, if you say this room is 20 degrees and the air is evenly spread, you know what it feels like, but you do not know exactly where every single air molecule is and how fast it is moving. There are an enormous number of different microscopic arrangements that would all feel like the same room to you. Entropy is higher when there are more possible hidden arrangements that still look the same on the outside. When things are very organized, there are fewer possible ways to arrange them, so entropy is lower.
8|11: You can think of entropy like the direction of time for messy events. If you watch a video of a glass falling off a table, breaking, and the pieces scattering, you immediately know which way is forward in time: the whole glass becomes broken. You never see shattered pieces suddenly leap up and form a perfect glass. That is because breaking the glass increases entropy. There are many more disordered ways for pieces to lie on the floor than ordered ways to make a perfect glass. The idea that entropy tends to increase is why we feel time moving forward, from more organized states to more disorganized ones.
9|11: Entropy is not about things getting dirty in a moral sense, it is about what is likely to happen when lots of tiny particles move around randomly. If something can be arranged in only a few neat ways but in millions of messy ways, randomness almost always pushes it toward one of the messy options. Entropy is just a number that tells you how many different microscopic arrangements fit what you see. Low entropy means only a few very special arrangements work. High entropy means there are a huge number of possible microscopic details, so it is very unlikely to ever go back to the special, low entropy state by chance.
10|11: A simple way to remember entropy is this: it is the amount of disorder or randomness in a system, and it almost always increases unless you put in effort from outside. Cleaning your room lowers its entropy because you are using your energy to create order. Cooking, burning fuel, using your phone, and even living all turn organized energy into more spread out, less useful energy. The total entropy of the universe goes up because every time we do something, some energy gets more spread out and less organized. So entropy is like a scoreboard telling us that overall, things naturally drift from order toward disorder.

1|14: Some of the cheapest mountains to climb are those that do not require special permits, guides, or expensive travel. In Europe, Mount Toubkal in Morocco is often cited as very budget friendly: flights to Marrakech can be cheap, the trailhead in Imlil is accessible by local bus or shared taxi, and you can do it in two days with basic guesthouse stays and simple food. In Eastern Europe, peaks like Rysy on the Poland–Slovakia border or Mount Triglav in Slovenia can also be climbed cheaply if you already live in or are traveling through the region, since there are no mandatory guides and public transport reaches the trailheads. Essentially, the cheapest mountains are popular trekking peaks in countries with lower costs of living and minimal permit requirements.
2|14: If you are looking for budget climbs, focus on non-technical trekking peaks in regions with inexpensive transport and accommodation. In South Asia, trekking up to places like Triund near Dharamshala or peaks in the Kuari Pass region of India can be very cheap thanks to low guesthouse prices and easy bus access. In South America, day-summits like Cerro Campanario or Cerro Llao Llao near Bariloche in Argentina, or easy volcano hikes in Ecuador such as Rucu Pichincha reached by public cable car, can cost almost nothing beyond basic food and transport. Picking mountains that start near towns and use existing hiking trails helps avoid costly logistics like 4x4 transfers or porters.
3|14: From a global perspective, some of the cheapest well known mountains to summit are those that locals treat more like day hikes than expeditions. In the United Kingdom, peaks like Ben Nevis in Scotland or Snowdon in Wales can be relatively inexpensive if you live nearby or find cheap transport, since there are no climbing fees and camping or hostels can reduce costs. Likewise, in the United States, non-technical fourteeners such as Mount Elbert in Colorado, when approached with personal gear and no guide, can be almost free aside from gas or a campground fee. So the cheapest mountain is often simply the closest non-technical one in your own country, reached with your own gear.
4|14: Cheap climbs tend to be lower altitude trekking mountains in countries with established backpacker infrastructure. In Nepal, instead of expensive expeditions like Everest, budget travelers often choose lower peaks and view summits around areas like Poon Hill or the smaller viewpoints off the Annapurna Circuit, which may have only modest permit fees and affordable teahouses. In Indonesia, Mount Batur on Bali and some less famous Javanese volcanoes can be done affordably if you avoid packaged tours and arrange local homestays and public buses. Thinking in these terms, the world’s cheapest mountains to climb are usually the ones integrated into everyday local tourism rather than specialized mountaineering.
5|14: One practical way to identify cheap mountains to climb is to look for destinations that meet three criteria: no mandatory guide, no high permit cost, and easy public transport. For example, in the Balkans, summits in Albania’s Accursed Mountains or Montenegro’s Durmitor National Park can be accessed by local buses and guesthouses, keeping costs down. In Mexico, hiking Nevado de Toluca can be inexpensive if you ride local buses from Mexico City and bring your own food and gear, paying only a modest park entry fee. These types of peaks give you big mountain scenery without the classic expedition price tag associated with iconic summits.
6|14: If you define cheapest in terms of total trip cost, then the answer is often the closest mountains with free access. For someone living near the Alps, easy peaks in Austria or Switzerland that involve only a train ticket and a picnic can be cheaper than traveling halfway around the world for a budget volcano. For others, modest hills like Table Mountain in South Africa, reached by city transport, can be one of the most cost effective classic climbs in the world. The key idea is that a mountain does not have to be extremely high to feel rewarding, and hiking local or regional summits will nearly always beat famous foreign peaks on price.
7|14: There are trekking peaks that are known among budget travelers as inexpensive summit experiences. Mount Sinai in Egypt, for example, is often climbed on a shoestring by backpackers staying in cheap accommodation in nearby towns, with simple food and no technical gear. In Central America, volcanoes like Pacaya in Guatemala or lesser known hills around Lake Atitlan can be done for modest guide fees and bus transport, especially if you join group trips. These are not high altitude technical conquests, but they offer sunrise views, lava fields, and dramatic landscapes without the heavy permitting or logistics fees of the Himalaya or Andes giants.
8|14: Cheapest can also mean the cost once you are already in a country. In Chile, many local hikers in the central Andes climb relatively easy peaks accessible from Santiago using regular buses, paying only for park entry, food, and perhaps a campsite. In the Caucasus, mountains around Kazbegi in Georgia offer impressive vistas with low daily expenses on homestays and shared taxis, especially if you stop short of fully technical ascents. By choosing routes that avoid glaciers and serious technical sections, you avoid the need for expensive specialized equipment, which can be one of the biggest hidden costs in mountaineering.
9|14: For people at the very beginning of climbing, urban fringe and national park peaks are by far the cheapest places to start. In Japan, famous mountains like Takao near Tokyo or even Mount Fuji can be relatively inexpensive if done independently during the main season, with costs mostly limited to train fares and basic lodging or even night buses. In New Zealand, popular tramping routes that summit modest peaks in Tongariro National Park or the Southern Alps foothills can be done cheaply if you use Department of Conservation huts or camp and already own your gear. These routes prove that iconic-landscape climbs do not always require an expedition sized budget.
10|14: Ultimately, there is no fixed list of the cheapest mountains to climb because your starting location and gear situation matter more than the peak itself. However, as a rule of thumb, look for non-technical summits below about 5000 meters in countries with low travel costs, minimal or no permit requirements, and frequent public transport to the trailhead. Mountains that fit this pattern include various trekking peaks in Morocco’s High Atlas, day hike summits in the European Alps and Balkans, accessible volcanoes in Mexico and Central America, and viewpoint peaks in India, Nepal, and Southeast Asia. Choosing these instead of branded expedition peaks will keep both your climbing dreams and your budget intact.
1|15: If you have a servlet filter mapped too broadly, it may be intercepting requests for static resources like images as well as your HTML pages. When the filter runs, it might change the response headers, encoding, or output stream in a way that breaks the image response, or it might block or redirect the request altogether. As a result, Tomcat never serves the raw image file, and the browser cannot display it.
2|15: Filters often assume they are handling text responses like HTML or JSON. If your filter wraps the response and writes text to it, or forces a character encoding such as UTF 8, it can corrupt the binary output that an image needs. The browser still requests the image URL from the HTML, but the response is no longer a clean binary stream, so the image cannot be rendered.
3|15: Your filter’s URL pattern might be matching everything using a pattern such as /* and then forwarding or redirecting the request to another resource instead of letting Tomcat’s default servlet serve the image from the file system. When that happens, the image link in the HTML is technically reachable, but the final response is not the image bytes itself and the browser treats it as an invalid image.
4|15: If the filter is doing authentication or authorization checks, it may be preventing unauthenticated users from accessing not only protected pages but also static images. In that case, the HTML is loaded because that request passed the filter, but subsequent image requests are rejected or redirected to a login page. The browser receives HTML or an error instead of image data, so the picture does not show.
5|15: A filter that modifies response headers like content type or cache control can accidentally override the correct image content type set by Tomcat. For example, if it sets everything to text html regardless of the resource type, the browser will think the image URL returns text content and will not render it as an image, even though the underlying data is correct.
6|15: If the filter uses a custom wrapper around the HttpServletResponse and forgets to delegate key methods like getOutputStream or flushBuffer correctly, the binary stream used for images may never be properly written out. The HTML still might appear because the main request uses a writer, but the image stream is silently broken or empty due to the buggy wrapper implementation.
7|15: Some filters read the request body or parameters and then consume the input stream without resetting it. While this is usually an issue for POST data, an incorrectly implemented filter could also interfere with the normal static file handling pipeline, causing Tomcat’s default servlet not to locate or serve the image resource as expected, making it appear as if the image link is unreadable.
8|15: If your filter relies on a specific application path structure and rewrites URLs, it can unintentionally change the paths to static files. When the image src attribute points to a URL that is then rewritten, the filter might generate a different path that does not exist or maps to a dynamic servlet. The resulting 404 or incorrect response means the image cannot be displayed even though the original path was valid.
9|15: Some security or compression filters wrap all responses and apply transformations such as GZIP compression or HTML rewriting. If such a filter is not configured to skip binary content types, it may try to compress or modify image data incorrectly or multiple times, corrupting the binary stream so that the browser fails to decode the image.
10|15: When running under Tomcat, static resources are usually served by the default servlet. If a filter’s mapping or filter chain prevents the request from ever reaching the default servlet, nothing will serve the actual file on disk. The filter might not call chain.doFilter or might end the response prematurely. As a result, the image URL exists in the HTML but returns an incomplete or empty response, so the image appears broken.
