1|59: For a simple but high-quality rose oil at home, start with very fresh, fragrant, pesticide-free rose petals. The more aromatic the variety, the better the result. Gently rinse and pat them dry so there is no excess water. Pack a clean glass jar about three-quarters full with the petals, lightly pressing them but not crushing completely. Pour in a neutral, stable carrier oil such as jojoba, sweet almond, fractionated coconut, or grapeseed until the petals are fully submerged. Stir with a clean utensil to release air bubbles, then top up if needed. Seal the jar and place it in a warm but not hot place, away from direct strong sun, for 2 to 3 weeks. Shake it gently every day to help extraction. After this period, strain through a fine cloth or coffee filter, squeezing gently. To intensify the scent, you can repeat the process by adding fresh petals to the same oil and infusing another 1 to 2 weeks. Store your finished rose oil in a dark glass bottle in a cool place.
2|59: To make a more concentrated rose oil without distillation equipment, use a repeated enfleurage style infusion. Choose highly fragrant, unsprayed roses, preferably picked early in the morning when their aroma is strongest. Remove the petals and spread them on a towel to remove surface moisture. Fill a jar halfway with petals and cover with a thin layer of a stable, odorless oil such as jojoba or camellia seed. Let it sit in a cool, dark cupboard for 24 to 36 hours, then strain and discard the spent petals. Immediately add a fresh batch of petals to the same oil and repeat the process. Do this cycle 5 to 7 times or more, until the oil smells strongly of roses. This layering of fresh petals gradually saturates the oil with aromatic compounds. At the end, filter carefully and optionally add a few drops of natural vitamin E oil to slow oxidation. Store in an amber bottle, tightly closed.
3|59: If you want something closer to true rose essential oil but at a small scale, you can do a basic steam distillation. Place fresh, clean rose petals in the top part of a large pot that sits over a smaller heatproof bowl. Add distilled water so it surrounds but does not cover the petals, and place a heatproof bowl in the center to catch condensate. Put the lid on upside down so that steam will condense and drip into the bowl. Put ice on top of the inverted lid to cool it and encourage condensation, then simmer gently for an hour or two. The collected liquid inside the bowl will be rose hydrosol with tiny traces of rose essential oil floating on top. Let it cool, then carefully skim off the microscopic oil layer with a pipette or small syringe. It will be a very small yield, but very concentrated. The hydrosol itself is also valuable and fragrant.
4|59: For an especially luxurious rose oil, combine both infused oil and a bit of purchased genuine rose essential oil. First, make a home infusion by packing a jar loosely with fresh or dried, fragrant, unsprayed rose petals and covering with a high-quality carrier oil like jojoba. Let this sit for 2 to 3 weeks in a dark place, shaking daily, then strain well. Next, boost the aroma using authentic rose otto or rose absolute from a reputable supplier. For facial use, you might aim for about 0.5 to 1 percent essential oil dilution; for perfume oil on pulse points, you could go up to around 5 percent if your skin tolerates it. For example, for 30 milliliters of rose infused oil, adding about 6 to 15 drops of pure rose essential oil will give a rich, complex scent. Mix thoroughly, allow it to marry for a few days, then use sparingly.
5|59: To emphasize both scent and skincare benefits in your rose oil, start by choosing your carrier based on skin type. For dry or mature skin, rosehip, argan, or avocado oil works well; for oily or combination skin, choose jojoba, grapeseed, or squalane. Fill a jar loosely with dried rose petals, which are less likely to introduce moisture that can spoil the oil. Pour the chosen carrier oil over the petals until they are well covered. Place the jar in a warm place, such as near a sunny window where it gets gentle warmth but not intense direct sun, for 2 to 3 weeks. Gently agitate daily. Strain through a coffee filter to remove fine particles. To elevate it, blend in a few drops of complementary essential oils such as geranium, palmarosa, or a hint of sandalwood, which all deepen the floral character. Test on a small skin patch before regular use.
6|59: If you are short on time, you can use a gentle heat infusion to make rose oil more quickly, though you must be careful not to burn the petals. Use dried rose petals to reduce the risk of moisture spoilage. Place the petals in a heatproof glass jar and cover completely with a mild carrier oil such as sunflower or sweet almond. Set the jar in a water bath: a saucepan with a few centimeters of water over very low heat. Keep the water hot but not boiling, around hand-hot to slightly hotter, for 2 to 3 hours. Stir occasionally. The gentle warmth encourages faster extraction of aromatic and beneficial compounds. Once the oil smells strongly of roses, remove from heat, let it cool completely, then strain through a fine filter. This quickly made rose oil is suitable for body oils, massage, or adding to bath water. Store in a dark, cool place.
7|59: To make rose oil that works as a personal perfume, lean into intensity and fixatives. First craft a concentrated rose infused oil by repeatedly infusing fresh or dried petals into jojoba, which has a long shelf life and feels light on skin. Do multiple rounds of fresh petals to increase scent. Then, to this infused base, add a small amount of strong perfume ingredients: a few drops of rose absolute for depth, a touch of vanilla or benzoin resin in oil for sweetness and fixative properties, and a subtle amount of woody notes like cedarwood, vetiver, or sandalwood. Aim for a total aromatic concentration of about 20 to 30 percent if using it as an oil perfume dabbed on pulse points. Let the mixture age in a tightly sealed dark glass bottle for at least two weeks, shaking occasionally, as the scent will round out and mellow. Adjust as needed after testing on your skin.
8|59: A very traditional method for making rose oil uses a form of cold fat extraction. Spread an odorless solid fat, such as deodorized shea or refined coconut, in a thin layer on a glass or ceramic tray. Press fresh, dry rose petals gently into the fat, making sure they are in full contact. Cover the tray to keep dust out and leave in a cool place for a day or two. Then remove and discard the spent petals, and add fresh petals to the same fat. Repeat this process several times over a week or two until the fat has a strong rose aroma. When finished, gently warm the perfumed fat just enough to liquefy and mix it with a neutral oil to thin it into a pourable rose oil. Strain out any solids. This method, similar to historical enfleurage, creates a soft, romantic rose fragrance with a vintage feel.
9|59: If you want a culinary rose oil for flavoring desserts or drinks, you must be very careful with both safety and taste. Use only edible, unsprayed roses, preferably known varieties like damask or heirloom garden roses. Gently wash and dry the petals thoroughly. Use a neutral, food grade oil such as grapeseed, light olive, or sunflower. Pack a clean, sterilized jar halfway with petals and cover fully with oil, ensuring no petals protrude above the surface, which can lead to spoilage. Infuse in a cool, dark place for about a week, shaking gently once a day. After a week, taste and smell; if the flavor is strong enough, strain through a fine sieve or cheesecloth and store in the refrigerator. Use fairly quickly and discard at any sign of off smells. This rose oil can lightly perfume cakes, pastries, salad dressings, or drizzles over fruit.
10|59: To get a really good rose oil, pay attention to every step from flower to bottle. Select the most fragrant, pesticide free roses you can find and harvest them early in the morning after the dew has dried but before the sun is too strong. Use only the petals, removing any browned or damaged parts. Decide whether you want a delicate oil for face care, a stronger one for perfume, or a culinary version and choose your carrier accordingly. Infuse slowly at low or room temperature to preserve delicate aroma molecules, and strain thoroughly through fine filters to remove plant particles that can speed rancidity. If you need more strength, repeat infusion rounds with fresh petals rather than overheating the oil. Store the finished rose oil in small, dark glass bottles filled almost to the top to reduce air exposure, and keep them in a cool, dry place. With fresh roses and patience, you will get a beautifully scented, nuanced oil.

1|61: The safest way to eat raw meat is to start with the highest quality product and strict hygiene. Choose meat labeled as sushi grade or specifically intended for raw consumption from a reputable supplier that maintains a strong cold chain. Keep it very cold, separate from other foods, and use clean, sanitized tools and surfaces. Trim exterior surfaces that may carry more bacteria and consume it shortly after preparation. Even with precautions, raw meat always carries some risk, so people with weakened immune systems, pregnant individuals, young children, and older adults should avoid it.
2|61: To reduce risk when eating raw meat, focus on controlling temperature and contamination. Transport it home quickly and refrigerate or freeze immediately. Freezing at very low temperatures for an appropriate amount of time can help reduce some parasites, especially in fish and certain game meats, though it does not eliminate all bacteria. Thaw in the fridge, not at room temperature, and prepare it just before eating. Wash hands thoroughly before and after handling, use separate cutting boards and knives from other foods, and discard leftovers rather than saving them.
3|61: If you want to eat dishes like steak tartare or carpaccio more safely, start with a whole, intact piece of fresh meat instead of preground. Surface bacteria are more common, so searing or briefly blanching the outside before trimming and using the interior for raw preparations can help reduce some risk. Grind or slice the meat yourself with well cleaned tools, keep portions small and chilled, and serve immediately. Never use meat that is past its use by date, has off smells, or has been stored improperly, since no seasoning or sauce can make unsafe meat safe.
4|61: Professional kitchens that serve raw meat follow strict procedures that you can adapt at home. They rely on trusted suppliers, rapid cold delivery, and rigorous storage controls, often keeping raw meat at or below fridge temperature right up to plating. They maintain meticulous sanitation, including frequent handwashing, separate prep stations, and disinfected equipment. When attempting similar dishes at home, copy these standards as closely as possible and recognize that you cannot fully match commercial testing and oversight, so there will always be some residual risk.
5|61: Not all meats carry the same level of risk when eaten raw. Beef from reliable sources is commonly used for tartare or carpaccio, whereas raw poultry and pork are generally much more hazardous due to higher rates of pathogens like Salmonella and Campylobacter. Many health authorities advise completely avoiding raw or undercooked chicken and pork. If you choose to eat raw meat despite this, limit yourself to species and cuts that are traditionally used for raw dishes and sourced from suppliers who understand and support raw consumption requirements.
6|61: Cultural tradition can offer guidance, but not a guarantee of safety, when eating raw meat. Many cuisines feature raw beef, lamb, or fish, and those dishes have evolved handling practices to reduce illness. Learning authentic methods, such as how butchers select cuts for kibbeh nayyeh, yukhoe, or steak tartare, can help you choose fresher, leaner, and cleaner meat. Still, modern industrial farming and processing change risk profiles, so combining traditional knowledge with current food safety advice and local regulations is important.
7|61: Another way to enjoy something similar to raw meat with less risk is to use lightly cured or acid treated preparations. Techniques such as briefly curing with salt, sugar, and spices, or marinating in acidic ingredients like citrus or vinegar, can lower surface microbial load and change texture and flavor. However, curing and acid do not fully cook the meat and do not guarantee safety, so they should be seen as risk reducing rather than risk eliminating. Combine them with strict hygiene, proper refrigeration, and high quality meat.
8|61: Personal health status matters a lot when deciding whether to eat raw meat. Individuals with chronic illnesses, compromised immune systems, liver disease, or gastrointestinal conditions, as well as pregnant people, young children, and older adults, are more likely to suffer severe consequences from foodborne infections. For them, avoiding raw meat entirely is the safest choice. If you are otherwise healthy and still decide to eat raw meat, treat it as an occasional indulgence rather than a frequent habit, and be extra cautious about sourcing and handling.
9|61: Communication with your butcher or fishmonger is a practical safety step for raw meat dishes. Ask specifically which cuts are freshest, how recently they were delivered, and whether they are suitable for raw preparations. A good supplier can tell you about their handling practices, refrigeration, and any freezing steps used to control parasites. Establishing this relationship can improve your access to properly handled meat. Still, remember that even the best suppliers cannot guarantee zero risk, so you are accepting some level of danger when you eat raw meat.
10|61: Ultimately, the only completely safe way to avoid foodborne illness from meat is to cook it thoroughly to recommended internal temperatures, which reliably kill most pathogens. All raw meat consumption carries some degree of risk, no matter how careful you are. Safe practice means focusing on risk reduction through sourcing, freezing where appropriate, temperature control, sanitation, and choosing lower risk species and cuts. Weigh the enjoyment of raw meat dishes against the potential health consequences, and make a conscious, informed decision instead of assuming that technique alone can eliminate danger.
1|62: Using secondary data involves analysing information that has already been collected by other researchers, organisations or agencies for purposes different from the current study. Typical sources include government statistics, large-scale surveys, organisational records and previous academic research (Saunders et al., 2019). One of the most significant benefits is cost efficiency: collecting primary data can be expensive and time consuming, whereas secondary data is often freely available or relatively inexpensive to access (Bryman, 2016). This allows researchers to allocate limited resources to deeper analysis rather than data collection logistics. Another benefit is the possibility of working with large, high-quality datasets that individual researchers could not feasibly generate on their own, such as national censuses or cross-national survey programmes (de Vaus, 2014). Using these large datasets can improve the statistical power and generalisability of findings. Secondary data can also facilitate longitudinal and comparative research because many data producers repeat measurements over time using consistent methodologies, enabling trend analysis and cross-country comparisons (Johnston, 2014). In addition, the re-use of existing data is increasingly viewed as an ethical and sustainable practice, maximising the value derived from data that has already been collected and minimising respondent burden (Corti, 2012). Nevertheless, researchers must critically evaluate the quality, relevance and potential biases of secondary data and ensure that the original context, sampling and measurement decisions are properly understood before drawing conclusions (Saunders et al., 2019).   References  Bryman, A. (2016) Social research methods. 5th edn. Oxford: Oxford University Press.  Corti, L. (2012) Recent developments in archiving social research. International Journal of Social Research Methodology, 15(4), pp. 281–290.  de Vaus, D. (2014) Surveys in social research. 6th edn. London: Routledge.  Johnston, M. P. (2014) Secondary data analysis: A method of which the time has come. Qualitative and Quantitative Methods in Libraries, 3(3), pp. 619–626.  Saunders, M., Lewis, P. and Thornhill, A. (2019) Research methods for business students. 8th edn. Harlow: Pearson.
2|62: Secondary data refers to information that was collected previously for another purpose but is now being reused for a new research question. This includes published reports, administrative datasets, academic articles, archival documents and commercial databases (Kumar, 2019). One major benefit is speed: because the data already exists, the researcher can move quickly from research question to analysis without the delays associated with designing instruments, recruiting participants and collecting responses (Punch and Oancea, 2014). This is especially helpful for students or practitioners working to tight deadlines. Another advantage is that many secondary datasets, such as national statistics or international survey programmes, have been collected with rigorous sampling designs and quality controls that might be difficult for individual researchers to replicate (Boslaugh, 2007). This built-in methodological robustness can enhance the credibility of a study. Secondary data also broadens the scope of what is researchable; for instance, researchers can investigate social change over decades using historical records or time-series data that would be impossible to collect anew (Hakim, 2000). Further, the use of secondary data can support triangulation, enabling researchers to compare their primary data findings with existing datasets to validate or challenge interpretations (Denzin, 2012). However, effective secondary analysis requires careful assessment of fit between the existing data and the new research questions, as well as transparency about any limitations in measurement or coverage (Kumar, 2019).   References  Boslaugh, S. (2007) Secondary data sources for public health: A practical guide. Cambridge: Cambridge University Press.  Denzin, N. K. (2012) Triangulation 2.0. Journal of Mixed Methods Research, 6(2), pp. 80–88.  Hakim, C. (2000) Research design: Successful designs for social and economic research. 2nd edn. London: Routledge.  Kumar, R. (2019) Research methodology: A step-by-step guide for beginners. 5th edn. London: Sage.  Punch, K. F. and Oancea, A. (2014) Introduction to research methods in education. 2nd edn. London: Sage.
3|62: In research, using secondary data means drawing on information gathered and often curated by others instead of collecting everything first-hand. This can involve official statistics, large panel surveys, organisational databases, historical archives, social media datasets or previous qualitative studies made available through data repositories (Bishop, 2007). A key advantage is access to scale and diversity: many secondary datasets cover large populations, multiple regions or long time periods, which allows more robust inferential analysis and the examination of subgroup differences that may be impossible with small primary samples (Fielding et al., 2017). Another benefit is that secondary data often come with rich documentation, including codebooks, technical reports and methodological notes, which can help researchers understand complex measures and design more rigorous analyses (Mauthner and Parry, 2013). From a practical standpoint, secondary data also reduce participant burden and ethical risks associated with new data collection, such as survey fatigue or sensitive questions, because the data has already been collected under established ethical protocols (Corti et al., 2014). Furthermore, re-using data encourages cumulative science: new researchers can revisit earlier datasets with fresh theoretical perspectives or improved analytic techniques, sometimes generating novel insights or challenging previous interpretations (Heaton, 2008). Despite these benefits, it is vital to remain critical about issues such as missing data, measurement validity and potential mismatches between the original and current research context (Fielding et al., 2017).   References  Bishop, L. (2007) A reflexive account of reusing qualitative data: Beyond primary/secondary dualism. Sociological Research Online, 12(3), pp. 1–14. 4|62: Secondary data use is central to contemporary social, business and health research because it leverages data infrastructures already in place. Organisations routinely collect transactional records, HR data, customer feedback and operational metrics, which can later be analysed for research and decision-making (Johnston, 2017). A core benefit is that secondary data can provide high ecological validity; the data often reflect real-world processes rather than behaviour elicited in experimental or survey settings (Biemer and Lyberg, 2003). For managers and policymakers, this can yield insights that are closely aligned with actual practice. Another advantage is the ability to perform retrospective analysis: by using archived records, researchers can reconstruct sequences of events, identify leading indicators or examine policy outcomes after implementation (Wooldridge, 2013). The re-use of administrative data in particular can support evidence-based policy by linking information across domains, such as education, health and employment, to understand complex social outcomes (Connelly et al., 2016). Moreover, secondary data allows benchmarking; organisations can compare their performance to industry norms or national indicators drawn from external datasets (Cooper and Schindler, 2014). At the same time, researchers must pay attention to data governance, privacy, and the original purposes for which the data was collected, to ensure legal and ethical compliance as well as appropriate interpretation (Connelly et al., 2016). 5|62: From a methodological standpoint, secondary data analysis is an approach where the researcher formulates new questions for an existing dataset and applies analytical techniques to generate fresh interpretations (Bosworth, 2010). It is not simply re-reporting published statistics but involves re-contextualising the data within a new theoretical or empirical framework. One benefit is that it can help explore hard-to-reach populations or sensitive topics where primary data collection would be difficult, risky or unethical, for example, research on crime, rare diseases or marginalised groups, where large-scale administrative data may already exist (Smith, 2008). Secondary data can also be vital in cross-cultural or international research; global indicators and large comparative surveys such as the World Values Survey or Eurobarometer allow researchers to study cultural patterns without conducting parallel fieldwork in multiple countries (Halperin and Heath, 2017). Additionally, secondary analysis supports teaching and capacity building: students can learn sophisticated analytical skills by working with real, complex datasets without the resource demands of new data collection (Dale et al., 1988). The efficiency of secondary data is not only financial but also temporal, making it ideal for exploratory studies or pilot projects that may later justify more targeted primary research (Bosworth, 2010). Still, scholars must remain cautious about over-interpreting results when they have limited influence over the variables measured or the sampling strategy used in the original study (Smith, 2008).   References  Bosworth, D. (2010) Secondary analysis of survey data. In: Vogt, W. P. (ed.) Dictionary of statistics and methodology. 4th edn. Thousand Oaks: Sage, pp. 395–396.  Dale, A., Arber, S. and Procter, M. (1988) Doing secondary analysis. London: Unwin Hyman.  Halperin, S. and Heath, O. (2017) Political research: Methods and practical skills. 2nd edn. Oxford: Oxford University Press.  Smith, E. (2008) Using secondary data in educational and social research. Maidenhead: Open University Press.
6|62: Using secondary data means basing a study on information that has already been gathered by others, whether that is official statistics, prior survey data, qualitative interview transcripts or digital trace data. In applied fields like marketing or public health, secondary data is often the first port of call when scoping a problem, understanding context or sizing a market (Malhotra et al., 2017). Its main benefit here is that it helps to clarify research questions and refine hypotheses before committing resources to primary research. For instance, analysts can identify existing patterns, gaps and anomalies in available data, which in turn guides the design of more focused surveys or experiments (Bryman and Bell, 2015). Another advantage is the potential for replication and validation: because secondary data are available to multiple researchers, findings based on those data can be replicated, which strengthens the reliability of knowledge within a field (Open Science Collaboration, 2015). In many cases, secondary data are stored in public or institutional repositories with standardised formats, such as the UK Data Service or ICPSR, which enhances transparency and supports data sharing (Corti et al., 2014). Moreover, when time series or panel datasets are used, secondary analysis can reveal dynamic processes, such as customer loyalty over time or the diffusion of health behaviours, that cross-sectional primary data may miss (Hsiao, 2014). Nonetheless, secondary data users must be transparent about data provenance, cleaning decisions and any transformations they apply, as these choices can significantly shape results (Malhotra et al., 2017).   References  Bryman, A. and Bell, E. (2015) Business research methods. 4th edn. Oxford: Oxford University Press.  Corti, L., Van den Eynden, V., Bishop, L. and Woollard, M. 7|62: In many disciplines, secondary data is the backbone of evidence synthesis and meta-analytical work. Researchers draw upon previously collected and often published datasets to aggregate results, identify broader patterns and estimate overall effect sizes across many contexts (Cooper, 2017). A notable benefit is efficiency at scale: instead of running dozens of new primary studies, meta-analysts can draw on the combined informational value of existing work, which both speeds up knowledge accumulation and reduces duplication (Lipsey and Wilson, 2001). Secondary data can also improve external validity because pooled information tends to span different populations, settings and time periods, making conclusions more generalisable (Borenstein et al., 2009). In public policy and health, systematic reviews and secondary data analyses inform guidelines, resource allocation and interventions by summarising what is already known (Petticrew and Roberts, 2006). Another benefit is that secondary datasets can be re-analysed using more advanced statistical or computational techniques than were available at the time of original collection, such as multilevel modelling or machine learning, potentially revealing new relationships or correcting earlier biases (Athey and Imbens, 2017). This contributes to methodological progress without new data collection. However, secondary synthesis is heavily dependent on the quality and comparability of the source data; issues such as publication bias, inconsistent measurement and missing documentation must be explicitly addressed (Cooper, 2017).   References  Athey, S. and Imbens, G. (2017) The state of applied econometrics: Causality and policy evaluation. Journal of Economic Perspectives, 31(2), pp. 3–32.  Borenstein, M., Hedges, L. V., Higgins, J. P. T. and Rothstein, H. R. (2009) Introduction to meta-analysis. Chichester: Wiley.  Cooper, H. (2017) Research synthesis and meta-analysis: A step-by-step approach. 5th edn. London: Sage.  Lipsey, M. W. and Wilson, D. B. (2001) Practical meta-analysis. Thousand Oaks: Sage.  Petticrew, M. and Roberts, H. (2006) Systematic reviews in the social sciences: A practical guide. Oxford: Blackwell.
8|62: Secondary data use is increasingly prominent in the era of big data, where digital platforms, sensors and online services generate continuous streams of information without any direct intervention by researchers. Under this broader definition, secondary data includes web analytics, social media posts, mobile phone records and other digital traces originally created for operational rather than research purposes (Kitchin, 2014). A major benefit is granularity: such data can capture fine-grained temporal and spatial patterns of behaviour that traditional surveys might miss, enabling, for example, real-time analysis of mobility or sentiment (Lazer et al., 2009). Another advantage is that these data often cover very large populations, sometimes near complete coverage for a platform or service, which can reduce sampling error and allow detailed subgroup analyses (Mayer-Schönberger and Cukier, 2013). From a practical viewpoint, researchers can exploit application programming interfaces and open data portals to obtain these datasets relatively quickly, though technical skills are required for cleaning and analysis (Kitchin, 2014). Secondary digital data can complement primary data by providing context or validation, such as checking whether self-reported behaviours align with observed digital traces. It can also be used in predictive modelling for policy or business decision-making, such as forecasting disease outbreaks or consumer demand (Lazer and Radford, 2017). At the same time, issues of privacy, consent, algorithmic bias and representativeness are particularly acute in big data contexts and must be carefully managed (boyd and Crawford, 2012).   References  boyd, d. and Crawford, K. (2012) Critical questions for big data. Information, Communication & Society, 15(5), pp. 662–679.  Kitchin, R. (2013) Big data: A revolution that will transform how we live, work, and think. London: John Murray.
9|62: From an ethical and sustainability perspective, using secondary data has several advantages. Because the data has already been collected, re-use can minimise the burden placed on participants, particularly in communities that have been heavily researched or where participation involves risk or emotional strain (Hammersley, 2010). Ethical guidelines in many fields now encourage data sharing and secondary analysis as a way of maximising the social value derived from participants contributions (ESRC, 2021). Another benefit is transparency and accountability: when researchers base their analyses on publicly accessible or archived datasets, others can inspect, reanalyse and critique their work, which supports open science and helps identify errors or questionable research practices (Nosek et al., 2015). This is especially important in policy-relevant research where decisions may have far-reaching consequences. Additionally, secondary data can support inclusion by allowing researchers from institutions with fewer resources to engage in high-quality empirical work, thereby democratising research and diversifying the voices contributing to scientific debates (Corti and Bishop, 2015). Many repositories provide training materials and user support, further lowering barriers to entry. Nevertheless, researchers must ensure that secondary use aligns with the original consent agreements and legal frameworks governing the data, and that adequate safeguards are in place when working with potentially identifiable or sensitive information (ESRC, 2021).   References  Corti, L. and Bishop, L. (2015) Strategies in teaching secondary analysis of qualitative data. Forum Qualitative Sozialforschung / Forum: Qualitative Social Research, 16(1), Art. 8.  Economic and Social Research Council (ESRC) (2021) Research data policy. Swindon: ESRC.  Hammersley, M. (2010) Can we re-use qualitative data via secondary analysis? Notes on some terminological and substantive issues. Sociological Research Online, 15(1), pp. 1–7.  Nosek, B. A., et al. (2015) Promoting an open research culture. Science, 348(6242), pp. 1422–1425.
10|62: In practice, effective use of secondary data requires a structured process: locating relevant datasets, evaluating their suitability, gaining access, and then preparing the data for analysis. Sources include national data services, institutional repositories, government open data portals and commercial providers (Saunders et al., 2019). One major benefit for project planning is that initial desk research with secondary data can help refine research objectives, identify variables of interest and assess feasibility before committing to primary data collection (Robson and McCartan, 2016). This reduces the risk of designing studies that are redundant or impossible to implement. Secondary data can also help in constructing sampling frames or estimating required sample sizes based on existing distributions and prevalence rates (Bryman, 2016). For students and early-career researchers, working with secondary data provides a valuable opportunity to focus on developing conceptual and analytical skills, rather than being overwhelmed by the logistical complexities of fieldwork (Punch and Oancea, 2014). Furthermore, combining multiple secondary datasets through data linkage can create richer analytical resources that integrate, for example, socio-demographic information with health outcomes or educational achievement (Connelly et al., 2016). The main challenges are technical and conceptual: managing different formats, definitions and quality levels, and ensuring that the integrated data still meaningfully represent the phenomena under study. When these issues are addressed, the benefits of secondary data use for efficient, robust and impactful research are substantial.   References  Bryman, A. (2016) Social research methods. 5th edn. 1|65: Identify a UK university that explicitly offers a PhD by Published Work route, then carefully align what you already have with their criteria. Start by making a spreadsheet of your publications: journal articles, conference papers, book chapters, patents, or practice-based outputs. Then compare these against each institution’s minimum requirements for quantity, quality, and coherence of publications. Once you find the institution where your existing work most clearly meets or nearly meets their standards, contact the postgraduate research office or a potential supervisor with a concise CV and publication list. Ask directly whether your existing outputs look suitable for a PhD by Publication and what gaps you might need to fill. Focusing on a university whose criteria you already meet, and where there is a sympathetic potential supervisor in your area, is usually the least painful route, because you avoid years of new research and concentrate on writing a synthesising commentary and navigating the formal process.
2|65: The easiest practical pathway is to treat the PhD by prior publication as essentially a packaging and narrative exercise rather than a new research project. First, confirm you have a body of peer-reviewed work that already demonstrates an original contribution to knowledge in a clearly defined field, usually at least 5–8 solid outputs at good levels of quality. Then pick a UK institution that offers PhD by Published Work not just to existing staff but to external candidates, and study its regulations in detail: word limits for the critical commentary, time-in-registration requirements, fees, and examination procedures. Draft a short proposal that explains how your existing publications form a coherent whole: the central research question, methods, findings, and impact. Approach a potential supervisor whose own research overlaps strongly with your body of work; if they are willing to support your application, the process becomes more straightforward and you are mostly formalising what you have already done.
3|65: One of the least burdensome routes is often via a university where you already have a relationship, such as an institution where you have taught, collaborated, or completed a previous degree. Many UK universities reserve the PhD by Published Work route primarily for internal candidates or staff. If you are or can become an associate or fractional member of staff, the requirements can be easier to navigate: you may get clearer guidance, better informal mentoring, and sometimes reduced fees. In this case, you would first secure an affiliation, then ask HR or the research degrees office about internal guidelines for staff PhDs by publication. With institutional support, you focus on preparing the integrative commentary that binds your publications together, ensuring they address a unified theme, and you rely on existing collegial networks to help you through the internal approvals and upgrade stages with minimal friction.
4|65: If your aim is to minimise extra work, you should start from your strongest existing publication cluster and reverse-engineer a PhD project from it. Group your publications by topic and pick the cluster where you have the most high-quality, peer-reviewed outputs that already show a clear narrative arc: initial problem, methodology, extensions, and implications. Then, filter UK universities by discipline strength in that area and look for ones that explicitly list PhD by Published Work, PhD by Published Portfolio, or PhD by Existing Published or Creative Work in their regulations. When you inquire, present a brief narrative statement describing your main research contribution plus a tidy list of publications that support that contribution. The easier path is where a potential supervisor immediately recognises a coherent story in what you have done; they can then guide you in writing the critical commentary that bridges your papers and shows how they collectively meet doctoral standards.
5|65: A straightforward strategy is to prioritise institutions with transparent, generous published criteria for prior publication routes and minimal residency or taught requirements. Some UK universities have very detailed online regulations, including exact expectations for number of papers, typical venues, and length and structure of the accompanying critical commentary. Make a shortlist of such universities and examine whether they accept external candidates, what their registration period is, and whether they require you to take research methods modules. The ‘easiest’ case will be where your existing outputs meet or exceed their baseline at the outset, you only need to be enrolled for a short time (sometimes one to two years part-time), and there is no requirement to generate new primary data. In such a context, the majority of your effort goes into the commentary, which you can often draft relatively quickly by synthesising and reflecting on work you have already completed.
6|65: From a workflow perspective, the path of least resistance is to treat the PhD by prior publication as primarily an editorial and reflective task. You do not try to collect more data or drastically expand the research; instead, you focus on curating and contextualising. Once admitted to a suitable UK programme, you structure the thesis around a critical commentary that explains the original contribution, methodological coherence, theoretical framing, and cumulative impact of your published work. You link each publication explicitly to specific elements of the doctoral criteria: originality, significance, and rigour. You also address limitations and future directions, demonstrating mature critical reflection. Since the research itself is done, you can often finish within the minimum submission period if you manage your time well. The procedure becomes easiest when you discipline yourself to write the commentary as you would a long review article about your own work, guided closely by the exam criteria.
7|65: For some applicants, the easiest way is to aim for a professional doctorate by publication in fields like education, business, health, or creative practice that recognise substantial practice-based outputs as research. These programmes may have slightly more flexible views of what counts as a contribution: policy reports, technical standards, professional guidelines, or creative portfolios can be acceptable, provided they are well documented and publicly accessible. In such routes, your existing professional outputs become the main evidence, and the doctorate emphasises critical reflection on practice and impact rather than purely theoretical innovation. If your career has already generated substantial, citable work of this kind, choosing a professional doctorate by publication at a UK university can be considerably easier than trying to reshape everything into a conventional, purely academic PhD by publication in a traditional department.
8|65: To maximise ease, you should be brutally realistic about where your current publications will be most competitive. If your work is published in mid-tier but reputable venues rather than elite journals, aim for institutions whose expectations align with that. An otherwise suitable route can become very difficult if you aim at a university whose unwritten norm is multiple top quartile journal papers. Survey recent successful PhD by Publication theses in your field from different UK universities: many repositories allow you to search by award type. Compare their publication lists and commentary structure to your own record. Choose the institution where your outputs most closely resemble those precedents. By matching at this level of realism, the process becomes far easier because you are not chasing an unspoken standard that your portfolio cannot match; you are instead fitting into a pattern that has demonstrably worked for others.
9|65: Logistically, the easiest experience usually comes from thoroughly understanding the administrative and examination steps before you apply and designing your path around them. Once you identify a promising UK university, obtain the full regulations, forms, and guidance notes for PhDs by prior publication. Map each requirement to something you already have or will produce: ethical approvals where necessary, evidence of authorship, statements about your contribution to coauthored works, confirmation of publication status, and any required training modules. Prepare these documents in advance, including signed coauthor statements clarifying your role if needed, so that your application and eventual thesis submission are not delayed by paperwork. With all the documentation ready and your publications already in place, the process becomes more about procedural compliance and less about open-ended research, which is generally the easiest kind of doctorate pathway to manage while working.
10|65: Another relatively easy route is to combine the PhD by publication with your current employment so that much of the time you would otherwise spend on new research is instead counted as part of your normal job. If you work in a research-intensive organisation, an NHS trust, an industrial R and D lab, or a policy institute, seek a UK university willing to formalise your existing publications and ongoing projects into a doctoral plan. The university supervises you primarily on synthesis and academic framing, while your employer continues to support the underlying research activity and possibly provide time or resources. This triadic arrangement can significantly reduce friction: you do not step out of your career to do a PhD; you formalise your career outputs as the basis of one. The ‘easiest’ aspect here is psychological and practical: you are not starting something new but reinterpreting and structuring what you are already successfully doing.
1|66: Model your slide as a simple curve and then use a camera with a Follow Path constraint. Add a Bezier curve that matches the shape of the slide (or is slightly above it), position the camera at the start of the curve, then add a Follow Path constraint targeting that curve. Animate the curve path by keyframing the Offset or Evaluation Time so the camera moves smoothly along the curve, giving a natural sliding motion down the slide.
2|66: Use the slide mesh itself as a path by converting an edge of the slide to a curve and then attaching the camera to it. Go into Edit Mode on the slide, select the central edge loop that runs down the slide, duplicate it, then separate it into a new object. Convert that edge object to a curve, then add a Follow Path constraint to the camera that references this new curve. This way the camera follows the exact contour of your slide without manually drawing a path.
3|66: Create a basic slide using a curve and an array modifier, and at the same time use that same curve to drive the camera motion. Add a curve, model the slide profile, then use curve and array modifiers on a mesh to form the slide. Parent the camera to an Empty, add a Follow Path constraint to the Empty using the same curve, and animate the path. Adjust the camera’s local rotation for the correct viewpoint while sliding.
4|66: For a quick result, use the Walk Navigation or Fly Navigation to manually record a camera move. Enter camera view, enable Walk or Fly Navigation, move down the slide like a first person game, and record the motion by keyframing at intervals or using the Record option. It’s less precise than a path constraint but very fast for a rough, organic sliding movement.
5|66: Use a rigged camera with physics-inspired motion. Place the camera just above the slide, parent it to a small rigid body object (like a cube or sphere) that sits on the slide, and enable Rigid Body physics. Let the object roll or slide down the slide while the camera follows. Once the simulation looks good, bake the rigid body animation and fine tune the camera’s rotation to simulate the feeling of sliding, including little bumps and tilts.
6|66: Combine a Follow Path constraint with a Track To constraint for a more immersive feel. Attach the camera to a curve that follows the slide using Follow Path, then add a Track To constraint targeting a specific empty located slightly ahead on the path or near interesting scene elements. Animate the target empty if necessary, so the camera looks around while sliding down, making the movement feel more dynamic than simply looking straight forward.
7|66: Use a camera on a rail setup. Create two curves: one for the camera’s position path and another for where the camera is looking. Constrain a camera rig empty to follow the first curve, and a target empty to follow the second curve. Parent the camera to the rig empty and add a Track To constraint aimed at the target empty. Animate both curves so the camera both moves down the slide and shifts its gaze in a controlled way.
8|66: Leverage the Follow Path constraint plus ease controls in the Graph Editor for realistic acceleration. After setting up the camera with a Follow Path constraint on a slide-shaped curve, animate the offset from 0 to 1 over the timeline. Then open the Graph Editor, select the offset curve, and adjust the handles to ease in at the top (slow start) and ease out or speed up in the middle to mimic gravity pulling you faster as you go down the slide.
9|66: Create a game like preview using the Blender Game Engine style navigation approach with viewport recording. Set up your slide and camera, then use a screen capture tool to record as you use Fly or Walk Navigation sliding down the slide in viewport. While this is not keyframe accurate inside Blender, it can be an easy way to get a natural looking slide motion for previs or reference before recreating it as a more precise path animation.
10|66: For a highly controllable setup, build a camera rig with custom controls. Parent the camera to an empty that handles rotation, then parent that empty to another one that handles position and follows the path along the slide. Animate only the main rig empty along the curve path, and add a few keyframes to the rotation empty for head tilt, leaning into turns, or subtle bobbing. This separates movement and orientation, making tweaks and iterations easier without redoing the entire path.
1|67: High-level migration path from Gwen to Myra in Intersect  1: Understand the current Gwen usage in Intersect Open the Intersect.Client project and search for Gwen-related namespaces and classes, typically something like Gwen, Gwen.Control, Gwen.Skin, Gwen.Renderer. Identify: - Where the Gwen renderer is created - Where the Gwen skin is loaded - Where GUI controls are instantiated - How input is passed to Gwen - Where Gwen Draw and Update are called in the main game loop Take notes of these locations, since each will need a Myra equivalent.  2: Add Myra to the project - Install the Myra NuGet package into Intersect.Client (for example via dotnet CLI or Visual Studio NuGet Manager) - Import the Myra namespaces in the files that will host the UI. Common ones:   using Myra;   using Myra.Graphics2D.UI;   using Myra.Graphics2D; - Initialize Myra at game startup, for MonoGame setups usually in the Game constructor or LoadContent:   MyraEnvironment.Game = this;  3: Replace Gwen renderer and skin Gwen expects a renderer instance and skin files. Myra does not use Gwen skins; instead it uses its own widgets and themes, usually loaded via .xks or .json layouts, or constructed in code. - Remove Gwen renderer initialization and any Gwen.Skin.Skin loading - Decide on a theming approach for Myra. For a minimal start, use default styles and focus on layout:   - Create a Desktop instance: Desktop = new Desktop();   - Desktop.HasExternalTextInput = true if you are integrating with your own IME or text system  4: Map Gwen controls to Myra widgets Look at each Gwen control you use and map it to the closest Myra widget. Examples: - Gwen.Control.Button  Myra.Graphics2D.UI.TextButton - Gwen.Control.Label   Label - Gwen.Control.TextBox TextBox - Gwen.Control.CheckBox CheckBox - Gwen.Control.ComboBox ComboBox - Gwen.Control.WindowControl Window - Gwen.Control.Menu  Menu and MenuItem - Gwen.Control.TabControl TabControl and TabItem - Gwen.Layout-related controls Grid, VerticalLayout, HorizontalLayout  Myra.Grid, HorizontalStackPanel, VerticalStackPanel Rewrite each UI construction section: - Replace Gwen control instantiation with equivalent Myra widgets - Replace Gwen property names with Myra ones, for example Text, GridRow, GridColumn, Alignments - Replace Gwen event handlers with Myra events such as Click, TextChanged, SelectedIndexChanged, etc.  5: Replace Gwen layout and parenting Gwen layout often relies on Dock, Margin, Padding, and layout panels. Myra offers similar concepts but the APIs are different. - For top-level UI, use a Desktop as your root. Assign a main container to Desktop.Root, usually a Panel, Grid, or other layout container - When replacing code that calls parent.AddChild(child) in Gwen, do the Myra equivalent:   - For Grid, use grid.Widgets.Add(widget);   - For Panels, use panel.Widgets.Add(widget); - If you used Gwen docking, consider replacing it with a combination of Grid and panels, or with Myras SplitPane or Docking where available in the repo version you are using  6: Replace Gwen input integration Gwen expects low-level mouse and keyboard events forwarded to it. Myra integrates with MonoGame through its Desktop object. - In the main Game Update method, call Desktop.Update(gameTime); - In the main Game Draw method, call Desktop.Render(); Make sure that the platform input hooks that previously forwarded input into Gwen are removed or no longer used. If you had a special input handling class bridging MonoGame input to Gwen, replace that to either: - Let Myra handle input via Desktop.Update and Desktop.Render (Myra automatically reads from MonoGame input APIs) - Or manually set Desktop.MousePosition, Desktop.MouseDown, Desktop.MouseUp, etc. if you are doing custom input routing  7: Replace Gwen resource loading If your UI was described in Gwen XML or loaded via custom Gwen skins, this will not be reusable directly. You have two options: - Rebuild all UI in code using Myra widgets - Or describe your UI using Myra UI JSON layouts built with MyraPad For MyraPad approach: - Install MyraPad - Design your UI visually - Export the layout as JSON - Load it in your code via something like:   var project = Myra.Serialization.Serializer.Load<Project>(stream or file);   Desktop.Root = project.Root; This can greatly speed up migration if you have many complex UI screens.  8: Porting specific Intersect UI screens Intersect has multiple GUI layers: login screen, character selection, in-game HUD, options menu, chat window, etc. Work through them one at a time. For each screen: - Extract all Gwen-dependent code into a dedicated class or region - Recreate it with Myra widgets - Check for event handlers that trigger game logic; make sure you rewire them to the new controls For example, a Gwen login button click handler:   loginButton.Clicked += OnLoginClicked; would become something like:   loginButton.Click += (s, a) => OnLoginClicked();  9: Iteratively remove Gwen from the project After you have each major UI screen running in Myra: - Remove Gwen references from the project file or NuGet packages - Delete Gwen-related adapter classes that are no longer used - Clean up using Gwen namespaces across the codebase - Try to build; fix any leftover references or types Aim for a clean build where Gwen is not present at all.  10: Visual and UX tuning Once functionality is correct, tune the visuals: - Adjust paddings, margins, alignments, fonts, and colors - Use Myra styles or custom themes if you want a particular look - Test different resolutions and aspect ratios to make sure the layouts scale properly  11: Testing and regression checks - Test all interactions: clicks, keyboard input, focus changes, text entry, scrolling, drag operations - Compare side by side with the Gwen version to ensure core flows are preserved - Test performance in heavy UI scenes (inventory, large chat logs, etc.)  12: Document the new UI architecture Finally, add developer documentation for the new Myra-based UI system so future contributors know: - Where the root Desktop is created - How to register new screens - Where to hook into game events from UI elements - How to use MyraPad layouts if you choose that path  This process is largely mechanical: systematically replace Gwen constructs with Myra ones, wire them into Intersects game loop, and then refine visual details once everything works.
2|67: Step by step Gwen to Myra replacement with focus on architecture  Identify Gwen integration points in Intersect - Open Intersect.Client and find the UI framework wrapper or manager. Usually there is a class that:   - Owns the Gwen canvas or root control   - Handles drawing Gwen each frame   - Routes input from MonoGame to Gwen - Note how GUI screens are structured: whether there is a ScreenManager or something similar that swaps Gwen containers in and out You want to replicate this pattern using Myras Desktop as the root.  Introduce a UI abstraction layer if it is missing Before touching Gwen directly, consider inserting one more level of abstraction: - Define simple interfaces like IGuiScreen, IGuiButton, IGuiLabel, etc. - Provide a Gwen implementation that adapts Gwen controls to these interfaces Once that compiles and runs, a second Myra implementation of the same interfaces will let you switch implementations more easily. If you do not want to introduce a full abstraction layer, at least group all Gwen-specific usages into a very small set of classes, like UiRootGwen, ScreenLoginGwen, ScreenGameHudGwen, etc.  Add Myra to the project - Install Myra via NuGet in Intersect.Client - In the Game class or equivalent entry point for Intersect.Client, initialize Myra   MyraEnvironment.Game = this; - Create a single Desktop field, for example   private Desktop _desktop;   In LoadContent or Initialize: _desktop = new Desktop();  Create a Myra-based screen manager - Create classes like MyraScreenBase or IMyraScreen; each screen will provide a root widget - Each screen exposes:   - A Widget Root property   - Load, Show, Hide methods - Your screen manager will hold a reference to the Desktop and assign Desktop.Root = currentScreen.Root - When you previously toggled Gwen screens, now switch Myra screens instead  Port one UI screen fully as a prototype Choose a small yet meaningful screen, such as the login screen, to prove the migration pattern. - Copy the login screen Gwen code into a working copy or side branch - Create a new MyraLoginScreen class implementing your new Myra screen interface - Create UI with Myra widgets:   - Use a Grid or Panel for layout   - Add TextBox for username and password   - Add TextButton for login and exit - Wire events to the same game logic as before - In your main game bootstrapper, swap the use of the Gwen login screen for the Myra one - Run and confirm that login works end to end  Define a style and theme strategy Gwen skins are often image-based; Myra relies more on drawing primitives, fonts, and theme files. Decide early on: - Do you try to visually mimic Gwen by creating Myra themes that use similar fonts, colors, and textures - Or are you fine with a slightly different look while preserving layout and functionality If you need theming: - Create a .json or .xks theme definition for Myra, or - Hardcode style properties in widgets and later refactor into theme files  Gradually migrate remaining screens With the login screen working, you have a pattern. Repeat: - Character selection screen - Main game HUD - Inventory and equipment windows - Chat box and system messages - Settings dialog For each screen: - Create a MyraScreen class - Use a structure of containers (Grid, StackPanels) similar in layout to what Gwen had - Transfer Gwen event handlers into Myra equivalents - Replace direct Gwen types in the rest of the client code with either:   - Accessors on your screen classes, or   - The higher-level UI abstraction layer you designed  Wire input and game loop just once One common mistake is to try to feed input to both Gwen and Myra at the same time. Instead: - Once a screen is fully Myra-based, remove all Gwen input routing from that code path - In the main Update method of the game: call _desktop.Update(gameTime); - In the main Draw method: call _desktop.Render(); Ensure you are using the same SpriteBatch and GraphicsDevice context that Intersect uses for drawing; Myra integrates into MonoGame via SpriteBatch.  Retire Gwen screen by screen For each Gwen screen that is now fully represented in Myra: - Remove references in managers and factories to the Gwen version - Delete the Gwen screen class or move it out of the build - Run the game and verify that no Gwen types remain in those flows Once all major screens are ported, you should be able to remove Gwen entirely: - Uninstall Gwen NuGet packages from Intersect.Client - Remove using Gwen; statements - Clean up any leftover adaptor or helper classes  Refine UX and performance With full Myra integration: - Optimize layout for different resolutions: test 16:9, 16:10, 21:9, etc. - Tweak fonts and sizes for readability - Ensure UI reactions are snappy: check that Desktop.Update is not doing heavy allocations or repeated layout rebuilds unnecessarily - Profile draw calls; heavyweight UI trees can be simplified using Grids and panels efficiently  Document how to add new UI features with Myra To help future work on Intersect: - Write a brief document or markdown file in the repo, describing how to:   - Create a new Myra screen   - Hook it into the screen manager   - Use MyraPad to design and export layouts   - Connect UI events to the rest of the client logic This shifts Intersect from being Gwen-centric to Myra-centric and avoids repeating the migration work later.
3|67: Pragmatic hands on guide: editing concrete Intersect.Client code to use Myra instead of Gwen  1. Set up Myra dependency Open Intersect.Client.csproj and add Myra via NuGet. From command line in the Intersect.Client project folder:   dotnet add package Myra Or through Visual Studio NuGet Manager. Then rebuild to confirm Myra is referenced.  2. Find the Gwen bootstrap Look for where Gwen is set up. Search in the Intersect.Client project for   using Gwen and likely classes like Gui or Interface. Common patterns involve: - A Gwen renderer based on MonoGame - A Gwen Canvas object used as the top level control - Calls like canvas.RenderCanvas or gui.Render - Input forwarding like gwenInput.ProcessMouseMove or similar Make a list of these entry points: they will be replaced.  3. Introduce a simple Myra root In the main Game subclass in Intersect.Client (often named IntersectGame or similar): - Add a field:   private Desktop _desktop; - In the constructor or Initialize method:   MyraEnvironment.Game = this; - In LoadContent:   _desktop = new Desktop();   _desktop.RenderCursor = true or false depending whether you want Myra to draw the mouse cursor This Desktop will replace the Gwen canvas.  4. Replace the game loops Gwen calls Find the Update and Draw overrides. They might currently do something like: - Update: pass mouse and keyboard states to Gwen - Draw: clear the screen, draw game world, then draw Gwen canvas Change them to something along these lines: Update:   // existing game update   base.Update(gameTime);   // at the end   _desktop.Update(gameTime); Draw:   GraphicsDevice.Clear(Color.Black or existing clear color);   // draw world and other content   base.Draw(gameTime);   // draw Myra last   _desktop.Render(); Remove the Gwen render call. You may keep Gwen temporarily for screens not yet ported by guarding use with flags, but the final goal is to have only Myra.  5. Replace a simple Gwen UI section with Myra Pick a concrete Gwen UI class, for example LoginWindow. A Gwen-based login might look like: - Constructed with a base Canvas or parent control - Uses Gwen controls: WindowControl rootWindow; TextBox userBox; TextBox passBox; Button loginButton; - Event handlers like loginButton.Clicked += LoginClicked; Rewrite it using Myra: - Create a new class LoginWidgetMyra with a Root property of type Widget - In its constructor build the UI tree using Myra types:   var panel = new Panel();   var userLabel = new Label { Text = User };   var userBox = new TextBox();   var passLabel = new Label { Text = Password };   var passBox = new TextBox { PasswordField = true };   var loginButton = new TextButton { Text = Login };   loginButton.Click += (s, a) => OnLogin(userBox.Text, passBox.Text);   panel.Widgets.AddRange in appropriate layout positions - Hook this into Desktop:   _desktop.Root = loginWidget.Root; Functionally, this is the same login UI, just implemented with Myra.  6. Pay attention to layout differences Gwen might have used docking and margins heavily. Myra uses: - Grid with Rows and Columns - VerticalStackPanel/HorizontalStackPanel - Anchoring and Alignments properties If a Gwen layout did something like: - Dock top menu - Center a window - Dock bottom status bar You can replicate with: - A main Grid with three rows: menu, content, status - Put a menu panel in row 0, the main content in row 1, status in row 2 - For centering windows, adjust widget.HorizontalAlignment and VerticalAlignment to Center  7. Convert complex windows incrementally For more complicated windows (inventory, character sheet, etc.): - First, sketch the control hierarchy from the Gwen version in comments: which panels hold which sections, how they are aligned - Then reproduce that hierarchy using Myra layouts - Copy event handlers as is and reassign them to Myra events For example, once you find something like:   itemSlot.Clicked += e => OnItemSlotClicked(index); Switch to:   itemSlotButton.Click += (s,a) => OnItemSlotClicked(index);  8. Integrate keyboard and text input Gwen might have had a specific route to handle text input across platforms. Myra integrates with MonoGames TextInput events. - Ensure that MyraEnvironment.Game is set so that Desktop receives text input - In some setups you might need to hook Window.TextInput and forward to Desktop:   Window.TextInput += (s, e) => _desktop.OnChar(e.Character); However, in standard Myra plus MonoGame usage, Desktop.Update reads from InputManager which hooks into MonoGame events for you, so try the default first. Test: - Can you type into a TextBox - Does backspace, delete, arrow navigation work  9. Remove Gwen infrastructure Once the main windows are ported to Myra and shown successfully: - Delete the Gwen canvas field - Remove Gwen renderer initialization - Remove custom Gwen renderers for MonoGame - Remove any Gwen input helpers Then remove the Gwen reference from the project entirely: - Via NuGet: dotnet remove package Gwen if it is a package, or - Remove Gwen project references and Gwen source files if they are included directly Search for Gwen in the solution to make sure nothing is left.  10. Optional: use MyraPad to make UI maintenance easier Maintaining large UI trees in code is tedious. To give Intersect a nice future proof setup: - Install MyraPad tool - Design each Intersect UI screen visually in MyraPad - Export to a .json or .xks file - In code, instead of manually constructing widgets:   var project = Myra.Serialization.Serializer.Load<Project>(file);   _desktop.Root = project.Root; - Keep the .json layout files alongside Intersect.Client assets Going forward, adding or changing UI is a matter of editing layouts in MyraPad and reloading, with much less C# boilerplate.
4|67: Conceptual migration blueprint: from Gwen centric to Myra centric UI in Intersect  Goal Transform Intersect.Client so that its GUI is built, updated, and rendered entirely with Myra instead of Gwen, without altering game logic.  Phase 1: Inventory of Gwen usage Survey the Intersect.Client codebase to create a map of current Gwen dependencies: - Find the core Gwen initialization: renderer creation, skin setup, root canvas - List major screens using Gwen controls: login, registration, character selection, in-game HUD, chat, inventory, dialog boxes, options, etc. - Identify helper classes that abstract Gwen (e.g. wrappers for message boxes, tooltips, etc.) Record this as a table: Screen  Login, Gwen root class  LoginWindow; Screen  HUD, Gwen root class  GameHud; and so on.  Phase 2: Decide replacement strategy Pick between two general approaches: - Parallel approach: Keep Gwen running and gradually introduce Myra screen by screen, controlled by a feature flag or config - Big bang approach: Build a new Myra UI layer in a branch and switch all at once when ready For a complex live project, the parallel approach is safer. Create a configuration option UseMyraUI; that lets you toggle between the two during development.  Phase 3: Establish a Myra core Add Myra to Intersect.Client and create a thin Myra core: - A single Desktop object that represents the currently active screen - A simple ScreenManager that owns multiple IUiScreen instances IUiScreen abstraction: - Widget Root { get; } - void OnShow(); - void OnHide(); - void Update(GameTime time); - void Draw(GameTime time); // may be empty if Desktop handles all drawing Hook this into the MonoGame Game lifecycle: - In Initialize or LoadContent: instantiate Desktop and ScreenManager - In Update: call ScreenManager.Update and Desktop.Update - In Draw: draw the game world first, then Desktop.Render This lays a foundation independent of Gwen already.  Phase 4: Model Myra UI with a design system Define your Myra design system so you can replicate Gwen screens in a consistent way: - Choose a color palette close to the Gwen skin - Define default font(s) and text sizes - Design common building blocks: TopBar, PanelWithTitle, ListRow, Tooltip style, ModalWindow style Implement these as reusable Myra widgets or layout helper methods; for instance - Create a method CreateModalWindow(string title) that returns a Myra Window with default styling - Create a factory method CreateLabeledTextBox(string labelText) that returns a Grid with a Label and TextBox configured By doing this before rewriting each screen, you minimize repeated work and keep the new UI coherent.  Phase 5: Convert one screen fully and refine the pattern Start with a simple but central screen, like character selection or login: - Study the Gwen version visually: what controls, what hierarchy, which events - Rebuild that using your Myra building blocks - Wire Myra events to the existing game logic - Map data binding: where Gwen code previously set label.Text or listBox.AddRow, now set the equivalent Myra properties or Items collections Once the new screen looks and behaves correctly, refine your helper functions and maybe introduce MyraPad layouts if desired.  Phase 6: Create Myra versions of all shared UI components Many Intersect screens share components: button styles, modal confirmation dialogs, list entries, tooltips, hotkey bars, etc. Identify all reusable Gwen components and port them as Myra components: - Confirmation dialog: previously Gwen WindowControl with two buttons; now a Myra Window with TextButton OK and Cancel - Message log entry: previously Gwen Label with color styling; now Myra Label or custom widget, with color coding - Pop up menus: map Gwen menus to Myra Menu and MenuItem Doing this early pays off when you migrate large screens like inventory or in-game HUD.  Phase 7: Screen by screen migration With the Myra platform and core components ready, methodically convert each screen: - For each Gwen screen S, create a Myra counterpart SMyra - Keep code that interacts with game logic as similar as possible, mostly just replacing Gwen specific property and event names with Myra ones - Integrate SMyra into ScreenManager Test flow: - Start client - Navigate to each feature in the game that uses S - Confirm behavior with the Myra version - Compare to Gwen version (possibly using side by side builds) to ensure regressions are acceptable  Phase 8: Input and focus edge cases Gwen and Myra handle focus and text input slightly differently. Check and adjust: - That pressing Enter in text boxes triggers the same actions as before (e.g. login on Enter) - That Tab navigation between fields is correct or at least not broken - That keyboard shortcuts used by Intersect (inventory key, chat key, etc.) integrate with focus rules: avoid shortcuts firing when typing in text fields if that was the old behavior Implement a small input service if needed to centralize rules like is UI capturing keyboard at the moment.  Phase 9: Remove Gwen from the runtime Once all end user visible screens are served by Myra behind the UseMyraUI flag: - Switch the default to Myra and use Gwen only as a fallback for debugging - Run full regression tests or at least a broad manual QA - Remove Gwen initializations and canvases - Strip Gwen input routing - Finally, remove Gwen references from the project and packages Run a final search for Gwen in the codebase to ensure it is fully gone.  Phase 10: Maintainability and future evolution The end goal is not simply replacing Gwen quickly but leaving Intersect.Client in a better state: - Document the new Myra based UI architecture with diagrams showing the role of Desktop, ScreenManager, and each screen - Provide short how to guides in the repository for contributors adding new UI or screens - Optionally create sample MyraPad layout projects for key screens so designers can modify UI without deep C# knowledge By following this conceptual blueprint, you not only trade Gwen for Myra but also create a more maintainable, well layered UI system for Intersect.
5|67: Minimalist technical walkthrough for developers familiar with MonoGame  Assumptions - You have Intersect.Client building and running - You are comfortable editing MonoGame Game subclasses - You know basic Gwen semantics and need concrete Myra replacements.  1. Add Myra to Intersect.Client - Use NuGet to add Myra to the Intersect.Client project Rebuild to confirm no issues.  2. Wire Myra into the Game class Open the core Game subclass in Intersect.Client (for example IntersectGame : Game). Add:   using Myra;   using Myra.Graphics2D.UI; Add fields:   private Desktop _desktop; In constructor or Initialize:   MyraEnvironment.Game = this; In LoadContent:   _desktop = new Desktop();   _desktop.RenderCursor = true; // or false if you draw your own cursor  3. Replace Gwen canvas draw with Desktop.Render Locate where Gwen is drawn; something like:   canvas.RenderCanvas(); or gui.Render(); In Draw: - Leave world drawing as is - Remove Gwen draw - Add at the end:   _desktop.Render();  4. Remove Gwen specific input routing Gwen often needs manual wiring of mouse and keyboard events. Search for code that sends: - Mouse position and buttons to Gwen - Keyboard key down and char events to Gwen Remove or isolate this logic. Myra via MyraEnvironment.Game and Desktop.Update will pull from MonoGame Input. In Update: - At the end add:   _desktop.Update(gameTime);  5. Build a new UI root instead of Gwen canvas Previously, top level UI likely had a Gwen Canvas or root control. Replace it with Myra widgets under Desktop.Root. Example skeleton:   var rootGrid = new Grid();   rootGrid.RowSpacing = 4;   rootGrid.ColumnSpacing = 4;   _desktop.Root = rootGrid; From here, everything is Myra widgets.  6. Straight mapping of common Gwen controls to Myra Where you used to write: new Gwen.Control.Label(canvas) change to: new Label() and add to parent.Widgets. Rough mapping table: - Gwen.Control.Label  Label - Gwen.Control.Button  TextButton - Gwen.Control.TextBox  TextBox - Gwen.Control.CheckBox  CheckBox - Gwen.Control.ComboBox  ComboBox - Gwen.Control.ProgressBar  ProgressBar - Gwen.Control.WindowControl  Window - Gwen.Layout.DockLayout / VerticalLayout / HorizontalLayout  Myra.Grid or StackPanels Events - Gwen Button.Clicked  Myra TextButton.Click - Gwen TextBox.TextChanged  Myra TextBox.TextChanged - Gwen ComboBox.ItemSelected  Myra ComboBox.SelectedIndexChanged You mostly only adjust names and event signatures.  7. Layout conversion recipe When converting Gwen layouts, follow this pattern: - Use Grid for multi row/column setups - Use VerticalStackPanel for stacked controls - Use HorizontalStackPanel for toolbars Example: login form layout Instead of Gwen VerticalLayout with children, in Myra:   var panel = new VerticalStackPanel { Spacing = 4 };   panel.Widgets.Add(new Label { Text = Username });   var userBox = new TextBox();   panel.Widgets.Add(userBox);   panel.Widgets.Add(new Label { Text = Password });   var passBox = new TextBox { PasswordField = true };   panel.Widgets.Add(passBox);   var loginBtn = new TextButton { Text = Login };   panel.Widgets.Add(loginBtn);   _desktop.Root = panel;  8. Replace one Gwen window at a time Take each Gwen based window class in Intersect.Client, for example InventoryWindow. - Create InventoryWindowMyra - Port constructor: where you used to create Gwen controls, now create Myra equivalents and build the same hierarchy - Event handlers: copy handler methods to InventoryWindowMyra and rewire events When done, stop creating the Gwen InventoryWindow and instead create the Myra version and expose its Root widget to be placed somewhere in the main layout.  9. Cleanly drop Gwen when done After all visible UI is ported: - Remove Gwen project references and using directives - Delete Gwen renderer wrappers you had for MonoGame - Ensure no leftover Gwen types remain Rebuild until Gwen is fully absent from Intersect.Client.  10. Optional quality steps - Introduce a small helper class UiFactory with common Myra widget setups to reduce repetition - If desired, adopt MyraPad to externalize large layouts and only keep code for event wiring and data binding - Slowly adjust spacing, alignment, and colors to get close to original Gwen visuals without over engineering at first.
6|67: Practical guide with emphasis on using MyraPad to replace Gwen UI in Intersect  Overview Instead of reimplementing Gwen UIs entirely in code, leverage MyraPads visual editor to speed up the transition.  1. Preparation - Ensure Intersect.Client builds - Note each main Gwen screen, ideally with screenshots, so you know what to recreate visually in MyraPad - Install MyraPad on your development machine from the Myra repository  2. Integrate Myra runtime into Intersect In Intersect.Client: - Add Myra via NuGet - Set MyraEnvironment.Game in your Game subclass - Create a Desktop instance and assign Desktop.Root to whatever UI you load from MyraPad Game class modifications example:   using Myra;   using Myra.Graphics2D.UI;   private Desktop _desktop;   protected override void LoadContent()   {       MyraEnvironment.Game = this;       _desktop = new Desktop();   }   protected override void Update(GameTime gameTime)   {       base.Update(gameTime);       _desktop.Update(gameTime);   }   protected override void Draw(GameTime gameTime)   {       GraphicsDevice.Clear(Color.Black);       base.Draw(gameTime);       _desktop.Render();   }  3. Extract Gwen layout information For each Gwen screen: - Look at how controls are arranged in code: docking, alignment, number of rows and columns, etc. - Sketch a simple diagram on paper, e.g. top bar, center panel, side panel, bottom bar - Capture font sizes, colors, and text from Gwen so you can replicate them in Myra You only need rough parity at first; visual fine tuning can come later.  4. Recreate layouts in MyraPad For each Gwen-powered screen - Open MyraPad and create a new project - Choose Root widget type (e.g. Grid for complex layouts, Panel or VerticalStackPanel for simpler forms) - Add child widgets that correspond to Gwen controls:   - Labels for text captions   - TextBoxes for user input   - TextButtons for clickable actions   - ListBoxes, ComboBoxes, etc. as needed - Organize them spatially to roughly match the Gwen UI - Adjust properties for alignment, margin, padding, and font size Once satisfied, export the layout as .xks or .json via MyraPads export functionality. Save each screen layout in a well defined folder in Intersect.Client, e.g. Content/UI/Myra/Login.xks, Content/UI/Myra/Hud.xks, etc.  5. Load MyraPad layouts at runtime in Intersect To use the exported layouts, add Myra serialization loading to the client. - Add using Myra.Serialization; - In code where a particular UI screen is created, instead of constructing controls, load the project:   using Myra.Graphics2D.UI;   var stream = TitleContainer.OpenStream(Content/UI/Myra/Login.xks);   var project = Project.LoadFromXml(stream); // or JSON equivalent depending on export   var root = project.Root;   _desktop.Root = root; Now the Myra UI for that screen is live.  6. Connect events and logic The layout alone does not include game logic. You must retrieve widgets from the Project and set up event handlers. In MyraPad, assign Ids to important widgets like loginButton, usernameBox, passwordBox. Then in code after loading the project:   var loginButton = (TextButton)project.Root.FindWidgetById(loginButton);   var usernameBox = (TextBox)project.Root.FindWidgetById(usernameBox);   var passwordBox = (TextBox)project.Root.FindWidgetById(passwordBox);   loginButton.Click += (s, a) => TryLogin(usernameBox.Text, passwordBox.Text); This replaces Gwen.Clicked events with Myra Click events but the underlying TryLogin logic can stay identical.
