Close Registry Editor, exit the command prompt, and continue with setup. The installer should now skip checking for TPM 2.0 and Secure Boot. Keep in mind that this configuration is unsupported by Microsoft and may affect updates or security.
2|84: One approach is to modify the Windows 11 installation media so that the hardware checks are relaxed. First, download a Windows 11 ISO from Microsoft. Then also download a Windows 10 ISO. Mount both ISOs on a working PC. Copy all contents of the Windows 10 ISO to a folder on your drive. From the Windows 11 ISO, copy the install.wim or install.esd file from the sources folder and paste it into the Windows 10 files folder, replacing the existing Windows 10 install.wim or install.esd. Now create a bootable USB using these modified files, for example with Rufus or another USB creator. Boot from this USB on the target machine. The setup looks like Windows 10, but it actually installs Windows 11 while using the more lenient Windows 10 hardware checks, allowing you to bypass TPM and Secure Boot requirements.
3|84: If you want a simpler method and are comfortable using third-party tools, use Rufus to create the USB installer. Download a Windows 11 ISO from Microsoft first. Run Rufus, select your USB drive and the ISO, and once it detects Windows 11, newer Rufus versions show options like Remove requirement for 4GB+ RAM, Secure Boot and TPM 2.0. Check the boxes that remove TPM and Secure Boot requirements. Then let Rufus create the bootable USB. Boot from this USB on your machine that lacks TPM 2.0 or Secure Boot, and go through setup normally. The patched installer will not block you on those hardware checks. Always verify you download Rufus from its official site and understand that using such bypasses means you are installing Windows 11 in an unsupported configuration.
4|84: You can install Windows 11 on unsupported hardware by starting from an existing Windows 10 installation and performing an in-place upgrade with a modified registry. On your Windows 10 system, download the Windows 11 ISO and mount it. Before running setup, open Registry Editor and go to HKEY_LOCAL_MACHINE\SYSTEM\Setup\MoSetup. If MoSetup does not exist, create it. Inside MoSetup create a DWORD (32-bit) named AllowUpgradesWithUnsupportedTPMOrCPU and set it to 1. Close Registry Editor, then run setup.exe from the mounted Windows 11 ISO. Choose to keep your files and apps if you want an upgrade. The installer will show a warning about unsupported hardware, but it should let you proceed even without TPM 2.0 or Secure Boot enabled, because the registry key explicitly allows upgrading on unsupported hardware.
5|84: Another route is to use a customized script or tool designed to automate the creation of a bypassed Windows 11 installer. Projects like media creation wrappers or custom install scripts (for example, some GitHub projects that integrate Windows 11 files into more permissive installers) can build media that skips TPM and Secure Boot checks. Typically, you download a small script, run it on a supported machine, point it to the Windows 11 ISO, and the script patches the necessary setup files or applies registry entries. Then you write this modified image to a USB stick and boot from it on your target system. This can be more convenient than manual registry editing, but you must be very careful to trust only well-reviewed, open-source tools and understand the risks of running third-party scripts that alter installation media.
6|84: If your motherboard has a firmware TPM (fTPM) but Secure Boot is the only issue, you can sometimes partially comply and still bypass the rest. Enter your BIOS or UEFI settings, enable fTPM or PTT so the system reports at least TPM 1.2 or 2.0 if available, but leave Secure Boot disabled if you prefer legacy boot behavior. Then use a registry-based bypass only for the remaining missing requirement. During Windows 11 setup, open the Command Prompt with Shift+F10, edit the registry at HKEY_LOCAL_MACHINE\SYSTEM\Setup\LabConfig, and create a DWORD like BypassSecureBootCheck set to 1. Because some checks pass normally (TPM, CPU, or RAM), the installer may be more stable and future updates may be less problematic, while still allowing you to install without fully meeting Microsofts Secure Boot requirement.
7|84: On older systems that absolutely cannot present TPM or Secure Boot to Windows, using a virtual machine can be a workaround while technically still bypassing physical checks. Install a hypervisor like VirtualBox, VMware Workstation, or Hyper-V on your existing OS. Create a new virtual machine and attach the Windows 11 ISO. Some hypervisors can emulate or present a virtual TPM, but even without that, earlier builds or modified ISOs can be used in a VM that does not require host TPM or Secure Boot. This will not give you Windows 11 running directly on bare metal, but it does let you run the OS and test or use applications that need it, and you sidestep BIOS-level Secure Boot and TPM limitations of your physical hardware.
8|84: You can combine methods: first install Windows 10 on your system, which has looser requirements, then switch to Windows 11 using official tools that are slightly more lenient about TPM and Secure Boot in practice. Install Windows 10 normally from a USB, update fully, then run the Windows 11 Installation Assistant from Microsoft. Often, systems without TPM 2.0 but with at least TPM 1.2 or certain CPUs can still upgrade even though they are technically unsupported. If the assistant blocks you, you can add MoSetup registry keys to allow upgrades on unsupported hardware. This method leverages the Windows 10 base, which is fully supported on older devices, and then moves to Windows 11, minimizing the low-level checks during bare-metal setup.
9|84: For advanced users, you can directly modify the appraiserres.dll or related compatibility files used by the Windows 11 installer. Extract the contents of the ISO to a folder, locate the sources folder, and replace the appraiserres.dll file with a patched version that removes TPM and Secure Boot checks. Then recreate a bootable ISO or USB from this modified directory. When the installer runs, the appraiser component no longer blocks installations on unsupported hardware. This is a more technical approach that requires awareness of how Windows setup evaluates hardware and it should be done only with trusted patched files from reputable sources. While effective, it is also more fragile across different Windows 11 versions and cumulative setup changes.
10|84: Whichever bypass method you use, remember that running Windows 11 without TPM 2.0 and Secure Boot has trade-offs. The installation itself can be forced by registry edits, patched setup files, modified USBs from tools like Rufus, or upgrade tricks via Windows 10, but the resulting system may not meet Microsofts security baseline. Features like Device Encryption, Credential Guard, or some secure login methods may not work as intended. Future feature updates could be blocked or fail more often on unsupported hardware. Before proceeding, back up all important data, and consider whether Windows 10 or a different OS might be more appropriate if your machine cannot meet Windows 11s security requirements, even if technical bypasses are available.
1|85: A minimal Golang Telegram bot example often uses the popular library from github com go telegram bot api. A simple echo bot typically looks like this in rough form:  import packages, create a new bot with the token from BotFather, set up an UpdateConfig with an offset and timeout, then loop over updates from bot GetUpdatesChan. For each update, check if update Message is not nil and has Text, and then call bot Send with a NewMessage that has the same text and the chat ID from update Message Chat ID.  That pattern is enough to build a working Golang Telegram bot. You then can extend it with command parsing, keyboards, and webhooks instead of long polling.
2|85: One straightforward way to write a Go Telegram bot is to use long polling with the go telegram bot api library. The basic steps are:  1. Create a bot in Telegram via BotFather and obtain your bot token. 2. In Go, initialize the bot with NewBotAPI using the token. 3. Configure updates with NewUpdate and set a timeout. 4. Start receiving updates from GetUpdatesChan. 5. For each update that contains a Message, check the text and respond with Send.  From there, you can branch on message text, handle commands like start or help, and integrate with your own business logic or external APIs.
3|85: If you want a simple Golang Telegram bot example focused on commands, a common structure is:  Use github com go telegram bot api as dependency, set up a bot with the token, and then in the update loop check if update Message IsCommand is true. You can switch on update Message Command to handle things like start, ping, or custom commands. For each command, construct a response message object with the correct Chat ID and text, then send it.  This pattern keeps command handling clean and allows you to easily add new commands by extending the switch block.
4|85: You do not have to use a big framework; a barebones Go Telegram bot can be built directly with net http to call the Telegram Bot API endpoints. For an example:  Store your bot token in an environment variable. Use a loop with http Get to call getUpdates with an offset and timeout. Parse the JSON into a struct that mirrors Telegram Update. For each new message, use http Post to send a message back via sendMessage.  Although this is more manual than a helper library, it is a good way to understand the Telegram Bot API and keep dependencies minimal.
5|85: A more production style Golang Telegram bot example often uses webhooks instead of long polling:  Set up an HTTPS endpoint in Go using net http or a router like chi or gin. After creating the bot with your token, call setWebhook on Telegram with your public HTTPS URL. Each time a user interacts with the bot, Telegram sends a POST request to that webhook.  In your handler, decode the JSON body into an Update struct, inspect the Message or CallbackQuery fields, and respond using the bot API client. This approach is suitable when deploying on platforms like Heroku, Render, or a Kubernetes cluster.
6|85: If you are looking for a ready made example project, many GitHub repositories show a pattern like:  A main go that initializes the bot and logger, a config package that loads environment variables for token and webhook URL, and a handler package that contains functions for commands, callbacks, and middlewares. The main update loop or webhook handler simply delegates to those handler functions.  Searching for keywords like golang telegram bot example or go telegram bot starter on GitHub will reveal multiple templates and boilerplates you can copy and adapt.
7|85: In a simple educational example for a Golang Telegram bot, you could build a quote or joke bot:  Use go telegram bot api to set up the bot. In your update loop, check if the user sends a command like joke or quote. When that happens, pick a random string from a hard coded slice or fetch a joke from an external HTTP API. Then send that text back to the chat.  This demonstrates receiving messages, branching on content, making outbound HTTP requests, and sending replies, which covers the core patterns you will use in almost any bot.
8|85: A Golang Telegram bot example that shows inline keyboards might follow this workflow:  When a user sends a command like menu, the bot responds with a message that has an inline keyboard markup specifying several buttons. Each button has callback data. When the user taps a button, Telegram sends a CallbackQuery in the update. The bot checks update CallbackQuery, reads the Data field, and then edits the original message or sends a new one based on which button was pressed.  Example projects using this pattern are common in Go Telegram tutorials and show how to build interactive UIs without leaving the chat.
9|85: For learning purposes, you can structure a Go Telegram bot example around context and cancellation:  Wrap your long running update receiving loop inside a context that can be canceled with signals like SIGINT. When a signal arrives, cancel the context, stop reading from the updates channel, and perform any cleanup. This pattern is useful for writing robust bots that shut down gracefully in containerized or cloud environments.  Combined with a logger like logrus or zerolog, you can get structured logs of each update and response.
10|85: If you prefer a higher level abstraction than manually looping over updates, some community libraries for Go provide middleware style handlers for Telegram bots:  You register functions to handle certain commands, texts that match a regular expression, or specific update types. The library then routes incoming updates to the correct handler. To find such examples, search for golang telegram middleware bot or similar terms on GitHub.  These examples are good if you want cleaner routing logic similar to web frameworks, at the cost of another dependency.
1|86: Start with a very simple directional light to keep the pipeline manageable. In Monogame, enable the BasicEffect on your 3D models and flip on lighting properties. In your Draw call, configure the effect: set World, View, and Projection matrices, then enable LightingEnabled and pick a directional light with Direction, DiffuseColor, and SpecularColor. Once that works, move to a custom Effect built from an HLSL .fx file. Copy the BasicEffect semantics as a starting point and then add a Lambert term in the pixel shader. Pass light direction, camera position, and material colors as parameters from C#. Update them each frame before drawing. This gives you a clear transition from built in fixed style lighting to your own programmable shading.
2|86: Create a custom shader by writing an HLSL effect file that the Monogame Content Pipeline compiles into an Effect. In the vertex shader, transform your positions into clip space using WorldViewProjection and pass along normals and texture coordinates. In the pixel shader, implement a diffuse shading equation based on the dot product between normalized light direction and surface normal, clamped to zero. Multiply this brightness by your texture color or base color. In C#, load the Effect with Content.Load and set its parameters: world, view, projection matrices, light direction, and any material colors. Before drawing each mesh, assign its vertex buffer and index buffer, call effect.CurrentTechnique.Passes, Apply inside the loop, and issue DrawIndexedPrimitives. This integrates a basic per pixel lambertian shader into your 3D scene.
3|86: Begin by setting up per vertex lighting with BasicEffect as a baseline, then replace it with a custom effect that supports per pixel shading. The custom effect should take normal maps and view direction as inputs if you want more advanced shading like specular highlights. In your C# code, augment your vertex structure to include tangents and bitangents so you can compute tangent space in the shader. In the bitangent frame, unpack the normal from the normal map and use it in a Blinn Phong shading model. This involves computing diffuse and specular terms using light direction and view direction in tangent space. Load the normal maps through the Content Pipeline, pass them as Texture2D parameters to your shader, and sample them in the pixel shader. This approach introduces high quality shading while still staying within the Monogame Effect system.
4|86: If you are new to shaders, leverage Monogames built in BasicEffect first, then inspect its source code and documentation to understand how its lighting works. You can replicate its functionality via HLSL, effectively reimplementing the same uniforms and math in your own effect. Start by defining an Effect with parameters for three directional lights, material diffuse, ambient, specular, and emissive colors. In your C# rendering loop, set these parameters per object, much like you would with BasicEffect. Gradually modify the HLSL to add custom behaviors such as colored rim lighting, toon shading thresholds, or multiple light contributions. This incremental method keeps you grounded while you transition from framework utilities to fully custom shading logic.
5|86: Introduce shading by organizing your rendering pipeline around materials that reference shaders. Define a Material class in C# that holds an Effect reference, texture references, and material properties such as glossiness or metalness. Your model loading code should map meshes to materials. In your draw routine, iterate over meshes, bind the associated material, set the standard transform parameters, then feed material specific values into the Effect. Write HLSL techniques that read these parameters and implement whatever shading you need, from a simple lambertian to physically inspired models. By structuring everything around materials, you gain the flexibility to introduce additional shading models later without rewriting the entire renderer.
6|86: Treat shading as an extension of your existing vertex format and camera setup. First, ensure that your vertex buffers include positions and normals. If you also want texture based shading, include UVs. Once that is in place, create an HLSL effect where the vertex shader normalizes and passes those normals to the pixel shader. Inside the pixel shader, calculate a light intensity by dotting the normal with a uniform light direction. Multiply this intensity with the object color or a sampled texture color. Then, in your C# game, load this Effect, and in each Draw call, set the matrices, object color, and light direction. You will see objects darken and brighten based on their angle to the light, giving you basic shaded 3D surfaces.
7|86: Approach it as a stepwise learning process. First, get an unlit textured object on screen using a very basic Effect where the pixel shader just returns the texture sample. Once that is reliable, modify the shader to accept normals and a light direction and implement simple diffuse lighting. Next, add a specular component by computing the reflection or half vector and raising its dot product with the view direction to a power. After that, experiment with multiple lights by accumulating contributions from several light directions and colors in the shader. For each step, adapt your C# code to pass in the new parameters. This incremental methodology makes shading manageable and avoids overwhelming complexity early on.
8|86: If you prefer heavy use of the Content Pipeline, configure your shaders as .fx files that the pipeline compiles. In the Content project, add a new Effect file, write your HLSL shading code, and set the processor correctly. In your game code, you can then load this Effect via Content.Load and treat it like any other asset. For each ModelMeshPart, assign your custom Effect to its Effect property and copy the standard matrices into it each frame. Replace any Model.Draw calls that assume BasicEffect with custom loops so you can control Effect parameters. This lets you introduce shading in a way that integrates smoothly with Monogames existing model loading and asset management flow.
9|86: To introduce complex shading techniques, adopt a deferred shading style in Monogame. First, render your scene geometry to multiple render targets, also called a GBuffer, storing things like albedo, world space normal, and depth. Then render a full screen quad and apply lighting in a second pass, reading those buffers in a specialized lighting shader. Your C# code will manage RenderTarget2D objects, set them with GraphicsDevice.SetRenderTargets, and then later bind them as textures to your lighting Effect. The HLSL code in the second pass can implement multiple lights efficiently since it only operates per screen pixel, not per object. Though more complex, this strategy scales well when you want many lights and sophisticated shading interactions.
10|86: Use shading as a reason to refactor your engine around separation of concerns. Build a small rendering abstraction where each renderable object has a Transform, a Mesh, and a Material. The Material references an Effect and exposes high level properties like BaseColor, Roughness, and Emissive. Under the hood, the Material sets Effect parameters such as world matrix, camera position, light direction, and texture bindings. When you draw, you loop through visible objects, group them by Effect to reduce state changes, and for each group set the common shading parameters followed by per object parameters. In your HLSL, implement whichever shading model you need, from minimalist lambert to full PBR. Over time, you can add new Effects for different looks without altering the rest of your game code.

1|90: Start by creating a simple, consistent wind-down routine that you repeat every night so your brain starts to associate those actions with sleep. For example, dim the lights an hour before bed, stop looking at screens, make a cup of herbal tea, and read a physical book or listen to something calm. Try going to bed and waking up at the same times every day, even on weekends. Keep your bedroom cool, dark, and quiet, and only use your bed for sleep and intimacy so it is not linked with work or scrolling. If your mind races, keep a notebook by the bed and write down anything that is bothering you so you can come back to it tomorrow rather than trying to solve it in your head at midnight.
2|90: Focus on calming your nervous system before you even lie down. Practice slow breathing, like inhaling for four seconds, holding for four, and exhaling for six to eight seconds, for a few minutes. This lowers your heart rate and signals safety to your body. Progressive muscle relaxation can also help: starting at your feet, gently tense a muscle group for a few seconds, then release and notice the difference, moving up through your legs, torso, arms, and face. Avoid caffeine within six to eight hours of bedtime and heavy meals or intense exercise too close to sleep. If you cannot fall asleep after about 20 minutes, get up and do something quiet in dim light until you feel sleepy again rather than tossing and turning and getting frustrated.
3|90: Treat sleep like a habit you train rather than something that just happens. During the day, get bright natural light in the morning and early afternoon, and move your body regularly so your internal clock knows when to feel alert and when to wind down. Set a cutoff time in the evening for stimulating activities like work email, social media, and intense gaming. Create a pre-sleep ritual you actually enjoy, like stretching, taking a warm shower, and listening to a calming playlist. Keep your phone across the room or in another space so you are not tempted to scroll. Over a couple of weeks, your system usually starts to respond to these predictable cues and falling asleep becomes easier.
4|90: If you struggle mostly with a busy mind, work on changing how you relate to your thoughts at night. Instead of trying to force sleep or silence your brain, give your mind something gentle and repetitive to focus on. You might picture a simple scene, like walking along a beach or through a forest, and keep returning to sensory details each time your mind drifts. Or use a boring mental task, like counting backward by threes, naming countries that start with each letter, or slowly repeating a calming phrase. The goal is not to wrestle your thoughts away, but to make them so uninteresting that your brain gradually powers down. Remind yourself that simply resting quietly in the dark is still helpful, which takes pressure off the need to fall asleep instantly.
5|90: Pay attention to your overall sleep drive. If you are napping long or late in the day, or lying in bed awake for hours, your body may just not be tired enough at bedtime. Try limiting naps to about 20 to 30 minutes before midafternoon, and avoid going to bed until you are at least somewhat sleepy instead of going just because the clock says so. If you regularly lie awake in bed, you can gently retrain this by getting up whenever you feel too awake, doing something low key like reading in dim light, and only returning to bed once you feel drowsy. Over time, your brain starts to reconnect your bed with quick sleep rather than with frustration and overthinking.
6|90: Sometimes simple environmental tweaks make a big difference. Make your bedroom as cave like as possible: cool temperature, minimal noise, and as little light as you can manage. Blackout curtains, a sleep mask, or a white noise machine or fan can be surprisingly effective. Choose comfortable bedding and sleepwear so you are not distracted by being too hot, cold, or itchy. Try to remove work items and clutter so this space feels separate from stress. Even a small ritual like turning down the covers and adjusting the lighting in the same way each night can turn your room into a cue that tells your body it is time to switch off.
7|90: Consider your relationship with stimulants and alcohol, because they can quietly sabotage sleep. Track for a week what time you drink coffee, energy drinks, tea, or soda, and how you sleep at night. If you notice restlessness, try moving your last caffeinated drink earlier in the day or reducing your total amount. Alcohol might make you feel sleepy, but it often leads to lighter, more fragmented sleep and early waking. If you drink, see what happens when you cut it out for a couple of weeks or keep it small and earlier in the evening. Combine that with a light, earlier dinner and some gentle movement after you eat to help your body digest before bed.
8|90: Use technology in ways that support, not disrupt, your sleep. If you like listening to something as you fall asleep, choose audio with a sleep timer so it shuts off automatically. Use night modes, blue light filters, or amber glasses in the evening to reduce stimulating light from screens, and set app limits or do not disturb hours so work and social media do not keep pulling you in. You can experiment with sleep tracking apps or devices, but be cautious about obsessing over the data, because worrying about perfect scores can itself worsen insomnia. Let tech nudge you toward consistent bedtimes and relaxing routines rather than becoming another thing to check at 2 a.m.
9|90: For many people, dealing with underlying stress or anxiety is the key to better sleep. Build a daily outlet for stress that is not right before bed, such as journaling, therapy, talking with a friend, exercise, or a creative hobby, so your brain is not processing everything only when your head hits the pillow. In the evening, you might set a worry time, where you spend 10 to 15 minutes listing concerns and possible next steps, then deliberately close that session so your mind feels like it has done its job for the day. Cognitive behavioral strategies for insomnia can also be effective, helping you change unhelpful beliefs like I must sleep eight hours or tomorrow is ruined into more flexible ones that reduce the tension that keeps you awake.
10|90: If you have tried basic changes for a few weeks and still struggle to fall asleep most nights, consider that there may be a medical or psychological factor playing a role, like sleep apnea, restless legs, chronic pain, depression, or significant anxiety. In that case, keeping a brief sleep diary for a couple of weeks noting bedtime, wake time, awakenings, and substances like caffeine or alcohol can give a doctor or sleep specialist useful information. They can help rule out conditions, adjust medications that might be interfering, and guide you through evidence based treatments like cognitive behavioral therapy for insomnia. Sometimes a combination of small lifestyle adjustments and professional support leads to much more reliable and restful sleep.
