1|96: In machine learning, a classifier is a model that learns to assign input examples to categories based on patterns in labeled data. You give it features, like pixel values of an image or words in a document, and it predicts a discrete class such as spam vs not spam, disease vs no disease, or which product a user is most likely to click. During training, it adjusts internal parameters to minimize errors on known examples, and then ideally generalizes to unseen data. Popular classifier families include linear models, tree based methods, kernel methods, and neural networks, each with different tradeoffs in expressiveness, interpretability, and data requirements.
2|96: Machine learning classifiers are algorithms that try to draw boundaries in some feature space to separate different classes of data. Imagine plotting your data points on a multi dimensional graph where each axis is a feature. A classifier learns shapes in that space — lines, planes, curves, or complex regions — that best divide the points according to their labels. Once trained, anything that falls on one side of a learned boundary is predicted as class A and anything on the other side is class B, with extensions to multiple classes. Performance is typically measured with metrics like accuracy, precision, recall, F1 score, and ROC AUC depending on the application and class imbalance.
3|96: You can think of a classifier as a function that takes an input x and outputs a label y from a finite set. In supervised learning, we observe many pairs of input and label and then choose the function from some hypothesis class that best fits these examples. Linear classifiers assume that there is a linear decision surface separating the classes, tree based classifiers recursively partition the feature space into regions, and modern deep classifiers compose many nonlinear transformations to represent highly complex decision boundaries. Regularization, validation, and careful feature engineering or representation learning are crucial so that classifiers generalize instead of memorizing the training data.
4|96: From a practical software engineering perspective, machine learning classifiers are components you plug into a pipeline: you preprocess raw data into numeric features, fit a model on historical labeled data, and then deploy it to score new inputs in real time or batch. Common libraries like scikit learn, TensorFlow, and PyTorch provide standard classifier implementations such as logistic regression, random forest, gradient boosting, support vector machines, and multilayer perceptrons. Engineering concerns include handling missing data, monitoring drift, retraining schedules, latency constraints, and integrating predicted probabilities with downstream business logic and user interfaces.
5|96: Classifiers in machine learning differ from regressors mainly in the type of output: classifiers predict classes, regressors predict continuous values. However, many classification models internally estimate probabilities, turning the problem into approximating p class given features. These probabilities enable threshold based decision making, calibration, and cost sensitive classification where misclassifying one class may be more expensive than another. Techniques like class weighting, focal loss, oversampling, and undersampling are frequently used to handle imbalanced classification problems where some classes are rare but very important, such as fraud or rare diseases.
6|96: Conceptually, training a classifier involves optimizing a loss function that quantifies how wrong the model is on labeled examples. For binary classification, losses like logistic loss or hinge loss are common, while multiclass classification often uses cross entropy. Gradient based methods adjust model parameters to reduce this loss iteratively. Overfitting is controlled with strategies like regularization, dropout, early stopping, and cross validation. Good evaluation requires splitting the data into training, validation, and test sets, or using k fold cross validation, to get an unbiased view of how well the classifier will perform on new unseen data.
7|96: Some classifiers are particularly interpretable, which is important in domains like healthcare, finance, and law. Decision trees can be visualized as a series of if then rules, linear models provide coefficients that indicate how each feature contributes to the decision, and generalized additive models offer flexible yet explainable relationships. On the other hand, highly accurate models such as deep neural networks and ensembles like gradient boosted trees are usually less transparent. To bridge this gap, post hoc explanation tools like SHAP or LIME estimate feature contributions for individual predictions, allowing practitioners to inspect why a classifier made a certain decision.
8|96: In real world applications, choosing a classifier is rarely only about raw accuracy. You also consider data size, feature dimensionality, sparsity, training time, prediction latency, ease of deployment, and hardware constraints. For small structured datasets, tree based ensembles like random forests or gradient boosting often perform very well out of the box. For high dimensional sparse text, linear models with good regularization can be effective. For images, audio, and other perceptual data, convolutional or transformer based deep networks dominate. A common workflow is to start with simple baseline classifiers, establish performance benchmarks, and then iterate toward more complex models only if necessary.
9|96: There are many flavors of classification beyond the standard single label case. Multilabel classification allows an instance to belong to several classes simultaneously, as in tagging a news article with multiple topics. Hierarchical classification respects a taxonomy of labels, like classifying animals into species, genus, and family. One can also do one class classification or anomaly detection where the classifier mainly learns what normal looks like and flags deviations. Furthermore, semi supervised and weakly supervised classification leverage partially labeled data, and active learning lets a classifier query for the most informative labels to reduce annotation costs.
10|96: Ethical and societal issues are central when deploying machine learning classifiers that affect people, such as in hiring, lending, policing, and medical triage. Biased training data or inappropriate modeling choices can lead to discriminatory outcomes across groups defined by attributes like race, gender, or age. Fairness aware classification introduces constraints or modified losses to reduce such disparities, and auditing classifiers using group specific performance metrics is increasingly considered best practice. Privacy and security are also concerns, as classifiers can leak sensitive information or be attacked via adversarial examples, prompting research into differential privacy, robustness, and secure deployment practices.
1|97: Look for a course that treats you less like a coder and more like a technical decision maker. A strong in-person option for senior engineers is a software architecture immersion that runs over several days and walks through real systems: decomposing monoliths, designing bounded contexts, making tradeoffs between different scaling strategies, and mapping business needs to technical designs. The best versions use your own problems as case studies, have structured whiteboard design sessions with critique from experienced architects, and include exercises around making and documenting architecture decisions, not just drawing diagrams.
2|97: A valuable in-person course for senior engineers is one focused on technical leadership and staff-level impact rather than specific technologies. You spend most of the time on skills like leading cross-team initiatives, driving consensus across stakeholders, influencing roadmaps without formal authority, and mentoring other seniors. There are role-play sessions for difficult conversations, group work on designing and pitching a technical strategy to execs, and practice writing engineering strategy docs and decision records. The environment should feel more like a leadership retreat than a coding bootcamp.
3|97: If you already know how to build systems, a deep, vendor-neutral distributed systems and reliability course can be transformative. Think of something taught by people who have built large-scale production systems, covering the practical realities of consistency models, failure modes in distributed systems, patterns for graceful degradation, and tradeoffs in storage and messaging technologies. The course should use labs and tabletop exercises where you diagnose simulated outages, design incident response playbooks, and critique existing architectures from real companies, so you get better at spotting fragility before it causes an incident.
4|97: For someone stepping into senior or staff roles, an in-person course on engineering management fundamentals can be powerful even if you do not plan to change roles immediately. The right program digs into planning and estimation at team and org level, mapping business strategy to technical roadmaps, performance management, and creating an environment where senior ICs can thrive. There should be practical modules on running effective 1:1s, designing career paths, giving growth-oriented feedback to senior people, and partnering with product and design. This helps you operate more effectively with managers and better understand how higher-level decisions get made.
5|97: A specialized security engineering bootcamp aimed at senior engineers can be incredibly useful. The curriculum might focus on secure design reviews, threat modeling, building secure defaults into platforms, and working productively with dedicated security teams. Expect hands-on labs where you analyze real architectures for security flaws, practice threat modeling with common frameworks, and get a grounded understanding of how modern application, cloud, and identity security actually fail in practice. The goal is not to turn you into a full-time security engineer, but to make you the person in the room who consistently designs safer systems.
6|97: Consider an intensive course on domain-driven design and collaborative modeling techniques. A good in-person version brings senior engineers, architects, and product leaders together and focuses on techniques like event storming, context mapping, and designing boundaries that reflect business reality. You spend a couple of days in structured workshop formats, exploring a sample domain or even your own company domain, discovering ubiquitous language, identifying seams for services, and designing integration patterns. This helps you bring alignment between business, product, and engineering and leads to more maintainable architectures long term.
7|97: For senior engineers working with data-heavy products, an advanced data engineering and analytics systems course can be worth the travel. The focus is on designing robust data platforms, data contracts between services, governance, observability for data pipelines, and real-time vs batch tradeoffs. You would do group exercises designing end-to-end data flows from product events to analytics and machine learning use cases, analyze failure scenarios like schema drift or backfills gone wrong, and learn how to communicate data architecture decisions to both technical and nontechnical stakeholders.
8|97: A practical course in technical communication and storytelling for engineers often has more impact than another deep dive into technology. Look for something where you repeatedly practice writing and presenting: architecture proposals, design docs, strategy memos, postmortems, and executive updates. The instructors should give detailed feedback on clarity, structure, and influence. You should present to the group multiple times, learn to tailor content to different audiences, and practice answering hard questions under time pressure, so you can make your ideas land in rooms full of leaders and peers.
9|97: An in-person workshop style course on legacy modernization and large refactors is especially valuable for senior engineers embedded in long-lived systems. The best programs use real codebases or realistic surrogates and walk through strategies for strangler patterns, incremental refactoring of critical paths, managing risk during migrations, and building tooling to make continuous improvement possible. There are sessions on communicating migration plans to stakeholders, quantifying risk and value, and leading multi-quarter refactor initiatives while still delivering features. You come away with patterns and language to tackle the hardest parts of long-lived software.
10|97: Finally, a cross-functional product and business strategy course that engineers can attend alongside product managers and designers helps you operate at a higher level. Think of a program where you learn how markets are analyzed, how business models affect technical choices, how to translate company strategy into technical bets, and how to argue for or against initiatives using both technical and business reasoning. Through case studies and simulations, you practice prioritizing a roadmap when resources are constrained, choosing which technical investments to champion, and articulating how engineering decisions influence revenue, cost, and risk.
1|98: In Python you can use requests and BeautifulSoup to recursively traverse the directory-style HTML indices and collect file URLs. For example:  import requests from bs4 import BeautifulSoup from urllib.parse import urljoin, urlparse  start_url = 'https://arcjav.arcjavdb.workers.dev/0:/001-050/%E4%B8%8A%E5%8E%9F%E4%BA%9A%E8%A1%A3/'  visited = set() file_urls = []  def crawl(url):     if url in visited:         return     visited.add(url)      resp = requests.get(url)     resp.raise_for_status()      soup = BeautifulSoup(resp.text, 'html.parser')     for a in soup.find_all('a', href=True):         href = a['href']         full = urljoin(url, href)          # skip parent links         if href in ('../', './', '/'):               continue          # basic heuristic: if link ends with slash treat as directory; otherwise as file         parsed = urlparse(full)         if full.endswith('/'):             crawl(full)         else:             file_urls.append(full)  crawl(start_url)  for f in file_urls:     print(f)
2|98: Using Node.js with axios and cheerio, you can recursively follow folder links and gather direct file URLs. Here is an example:  const axios = require('axios'); const cheerio = require('cheerio'); const { URL } = require('url');  const startUrl = 'https://arcjav.arcjavdb.workers.dev/0:/001-050/%E4%B8%8A%E5%8E%9F%E4%BA%9A%E8%A1%A3/';  const visited = new Set(); const files = [];  async function crawl(url) {   if (visited.has(url)) return;   visited.add(url);    const res = await axios.get(url);   const $ = cheerio.load(res.data);    $('a[href]').each((_, el) => {     const href = $(el).attr('href');     if (!href || href === '../' || href === './' || href === '/') return;      const full = new URL(href, url).toString();      if (full.endsWith('/')) {       crawl(full).catch(console.error);     } else {       files.push(full);     }   }); }  crawl(startUrl)   .then(() => {     console.log(files.join('\n'));   })   .catch(console.error);
3|98: In Python you can use an async approach with httpx and selectolax to speed up recursion over many subdirectories. The idea is to parse directory listings and schedule tasks for subfolders while collecting file links.  import asyncio import httpx from selectolax.parser import HTMLParser from urllib.parse import urljoin  start_url = 'https://arcjav.arcjavdb.workers.dev/0:/001-050/%E4%B8%8A%E5%8E%9F%E4%BA%9A%E8%A1%A3/'  visited = set() file_urls = []  async def crawl(client, url):     if url in visited:         return     visited.add(url)      r = await client.get(url)     r.raise_for_status()      tree = HTMLParser(r.text)     tasks = []      for node in tree.css('a[href]'):         href = node.attributes.get('href')         if not href or href in ('../', './', '/'):               continue         full = urljoin(url, href)         if full.endswith('/'):             tasks.append(asyncio.create_task(crawl(client, full)))         else:             file_urls.append(full)      if tasks:         await asyncio.gather(*tasks)  async def main():     async with httpx.AsyncClient(timeout=30) as client:         await crawl(client, start_url)      for u in file_urls:         print(u)  asyncio.run(main())
4|98: If the directory listing is API driven (for example a Cloudflare Workers KV backed index) you may be able to use an API endpoint rather than scraping HTML. Many directory indexers expose a JSON listing when requested with a query parameter. For instance, you might try something like  https://arcjav.arcjavdb.workers.dev/api?path=/0:/001-050/上原亞衣/  If that exists and returns JSON with fields like files and folders, you can recursively follow the JSON without parsing HTML.  In pseudocode:  function crawl(path):   json = GET(apiEndpoint + '?path=' + encode(path))   for folder in json.folders:     crawl(folder.path)   for file in json.files:     print(file.url)  You would adapt field names based on the actual JSON structure returned. This approach tends to be more robust than guessing from HTML directory listings, especially if the site changes its markup but keeps the API stable.
5|98: Here is a simple Python program using only the standard library (urllib and html.parser) to recursively pull file URLs from that base directory. It assumes the server exposes a basic directory index with links.  from urllib.request import urlopen from urllib.parse import urljoin from html.parser import HTMLParser  start_url = 'https://arcjav.arcjavdb.workers.dev/0:/001-050/%E4%B8%8A%E5%8E%9F%E4%BA%9A%E8%A1%A3/'  class LinkParser(HTMLParser):     def __init__(self):         super().__init__()         self.links = []     def handle_starttag(self, tag, attrs):         if tag.lower() == 'a':             href = dict(attrs).get('href')             if href:                 self.links.append(href)  visited = set() file_urls = []  def crawl(url):     if url in visited:         return     visited.add(url)      with urlopen(url) as resp:         html = resp.read().decode(errors='ignore')      parser = LinkParser()     parser.feed(html)      for href in parser.links:         if href in ('../', './', '/'):               continue         full = urljoin(url, href)         if full.endswith('/'):             crawl(full)         else:             file_urls.append(full)  crawl(start_url)  for u in file_urls:     print(u)
6|98: You can use a headless browser like Playwright to recursively enumerate directories if the page structure is dynamic or rendered by JavaScript. For example, in Node.js with Playwright for Chromium:  const { chromium } = require('playwright');  const startUrl = 'https://arcjav.arcjavdb.workers.dev/0:/001-050/%E4%B8%8A%E5%8E%9F%E4%BA%9A%E8%A1%A3/';  const visited = new Set(); const files = [];  async function crawl(page, url) {   if (visited.has(url)) return;   visited.add(url);    await page.goto(url, { waitUntil: 'networkidle' });    const links = await page.$$eval('a[href]', as => as.map(a => a.getAttribute('href')));    for (const href of links) {     if (!href || href === '../' || href === './' || href === '/') continue;      const full = new URL(href, url).toString();     if (full.endsWith('/')) {       await crawl(page, full);     } else {       files.push(full);     }   } }  (async () => {   const browser = await chromium.launch();   const page = await browser.newPage();    await crawl(page, startUrl);    console.log(files.join('\n'));   await browser.close(); })();
7|98: Instead of scraping HTML, you can leverage the WebDAV style directory structure that some workers.dev file indexes provide, using PROPFIND requests to recursively discover resources. In Python with requests you might do  import requests from xml.etree import ElementTree as ET  base = 'https://arcjav.arcjavdb.workers.dev/0:/001-050/%E4%B8%8A%E5%8E%9F%E4%BA%9A%E8%A1%A3/'  visited = set() files = []  headers = {     'Depth': '1',     'Content-Type': 'text/xml' } body = '''<?xml version="1.0" encoding="utf-8"?> <D:propfind xmlns:D="DAV:">   <D:allprop/> </D:propfind>'''  def crawl(url):     if url in visited:         return     visited.add(url)      r = requests.request('PROPFIND', url, headers=headers, data=body)     if r.status_code not in (207, 200):         return      tree = ET.fromstring(r.text)     ns = {'d': 'DAV:'}      for resp in tree.findall('d:response', ns):         href = resp.find('d:href', ns)         if href is None:             continue         full = href.text         if full == url:             continue          is_collection = any(             prop.find('d:resourcetype/d:collection', ns) is not None             for prop in resp.findall('d:propstat/d:prop', ns)         )          if is_collection:             crawl(full)         else:             files.append(full)  crawl(base)  for f in files:     print(f)  This only works if the endpoint actually supports WebDAV semantics, but some cloud storage proxies do, making recursive file discovery easier.
8|98: If you are using Go, you can write a recursive crawler that uses net/http and golang.org/x/net/html to parse the directory listings. For example  package main  import (     "fmt"     "log"     "net/http"     "net/url"      "golang.org/x/net/html" )  var (     visited = make(map[string]bool)     files   []string )  func crawl(raw string) {     if visited[raw] {         return     }     visited[raw] = true      resp, err := http.Get(raw)     if err != nil {         log.Println(err)         return     }     defer resp.Body.Close()      doc, err := html.Parse(resp.Body)     if err != nil {         log.Println(err)         return     }      var f func(*html.Node)     f = func(n *html.Node) {         if n.Type == html.ElementNode && n.Data == "a" {             for _, a := range n.Attr {                 if a.Key == "href" {                     href := a.Val                     if href == "../" || href == "./" || href == "/" {                         break                     }                     base, _ := url.Parse(raw)                     u, _ := url.Parse(href)                     full := base.ResolveReference(u).String()                     if full[len(full)-1] == '/' {                         crawl(full)                     } else {                         files = append(files, full)                     }                     break                 }             }         }         for c := n.FirstChild; c != nil; c = c.NextSibling {             f(c)         }     }     f(doc) }  func main() {     start := "https://arcjav.arcjavdb.workers.dev/0:/001-050/%E4%B8%8A%E5%8E%9F%E4%BA%9A%E8%A1%A3/"     crawl(start)     for _, f := range files {         fmt.Println(f)     } }
9|98: With Ruby you can use open-uri and Nokogiri to recursively walk directory pages and collect file URLs. Here is an example script  require 'open-uri' require 'nokogiri' require 'uri'  START_URL = 'https://arcjav.arcjavdb.workers.dev/0:/001-050/%E4%B8%8A%E5%8E%9F%E4%BA%9A%E8%A1%A3/'  $visited = {} $files = []  def crawl(url)   return if $visited[url]   $visited[url] = true    html = URI.open(url, &:read)   doc = Nokogiri::HTML(html)    doc.css('a[href]').each do |a|     href = a['href']     next if !href || ['../', './', '/'].include?(href)      full = URI.join(url, href).to_s     if full.end_with?('/')       crawl(full)     else       $files << full     end   end end  crawl(START_URL) puts $files
10|98: If the directory structure is relatively shallow and you only need immediate files and one level of subfolders, you can keep the recursion logic very compact. For example, in Python with requests and lxml  import requests from lxml import html from urllib.parse import urljoin  BASE = 'https://arcjav.arcjavdb.workers.dev/0:/001-050/%E4%B8%8A%E5%8E%9F%E4%BA%9A%E8%A1%A3/'  seen = set() files = []  def crawl(url):     if url in seen:         return     seen.add(url)      r = requests.get(url)     r.raise_for_status()      tree = html.fromstring(r.text)     hrefs = tree.xpath('//a[@href]/@href')      for href in hrefs:         if href in ('../', './', '/'):               continue         full = urljoin(url, href)         if full.endswith('/'):             crawl(full)         else:             files.append(full)  crawl(BASE)  for u in files:     print(u)  This is lightweight yet still fully recursive, and lxmls XPath support can make it easy to refine which links you follow if the directory pages also contain navigation or non file links.
1|99: Focus first on a real problem, then on your solution. Talk to potential customers before you build anything and keep talking to them as you iterate. Charge from day one, even if it is small; it is the fastest way to learn whether you are creating real value. Keep your costs low early, avoid vanity metrics like follower counts, and track only what ties directly to revenue, retention, or customer satisfaction.
2|99: Think of business as a series of experiments. Form a hypothesis about a product or market, test it quickly with the smallest version you can, measure the response, and either double down or pivot. Move fast, but be disciplined about documenting what you tried and what you learned. Over time, this habit compounds into sharp intuition and better strategic decisions.
3|99: Start by defining what success actually means to you, not just what sounds impressive to other people. Some businesses are built for growth and fundraising, others for independence and steady cash flow. Once you are clear on your goal, you can choose matching strategies for pricing, hiring, marketing, and financing instead of copying whatever is currently fashionable in the startup world.
4|99: Cash flow management is more important than profit on paper, especially in the early stages. Understand when money actually enters and leaves your bank account, not just your accounting system. Negotiate payment terms with suppliers and customers, keep a realistic cash buffer for surprises, and regularly check whether any new commitment you make is likely to strain your liquidity.
5|99: Build trust before you try to sell aggressively. Share useful knowledge freely, be transparent about limitations, and underpromise compared to what you believe you can deliver. When mistakes happen, own them quickly and make things right. A reputation for reliability and fairness will lower your marketing costs over time because word of mouth becomes your strongest channel.
6|99: Choose a narrow target customer first and serve them exceptionally well instead of chasing everyone. Specialization makes decisions easier: you know which features to build, where to market, how to speak, and what to ignore. Once you dominate a niche and have processes that work, you can expand horizontally into adjacent segments with far less risk and confusion.
7|99: Recruit people for attitude and learning ability more than for perfect resumes. Clear expectations, simple systems, and a culture where feedback is normal will outperform fancy perks. Document how you do things as you go, even if it feels slow; good processes turn individual heroics into something that scales and can function without you in every detail.
8|99: Marketing is about understanding, not just shouting. Spend time in the same online spaces as your customers, listen to their language, and use that exact language in your messages. Focus your energy on one or two channels that actually convert, refine your message there, and only then think about expanding to new platforms or tactics.
9|99: Price based on value, not on your insecurity. If what you offer saves time, reduces risk, or increases revenue, calculate a rough estimate of that value and price as a fraction of it instead of guessing. Test different price points; you may be surprised that slightly higher pricing attracts more serious customers and gives you the margin needed to serve them well.
10|99: Keep an honest scoreboard for your business. Pick a small set of metrics such as monthly revenue, new customers, churn, and customer satisfaction, and review them on a regular rhythm. Combine this quantitative data with a few deep qualitative conversations with customers, and let both guide your priorities so you do not get lost in endless ideas and distractions.
